The fluorescence reconstruction is `c + b`; spikes need not follow the fluorescence waveform. [OASIS definitions](https://github.com/j-friedrich/OASIS/blob/master/oasis/functions.py#L293-L304)
>

> c, s, b, g, lam = deconvolve(y, penalty=1, b_nonneg=False)

> ax[0].plot(c + b, color="orange", label="OASIS reconstruction")
> ax[0].axhline(b, color="gray", linestyle="--", label="Fitted baseline")
> ax[0].legend()
> ax[1].plot(s, color="red", label="Estimated spikes")
> ax[1].legend()
> ```
>

Strong or sustained activity raises that mean, potentially creating negative dips around events and suppressing sustained signals. That is a hypothesis from the code, not a diagnosis of the missing plot.

Estimate and subtract a slowly varying baseline beforehand—for example, test a rolling low percentile instead of a rolling mean. Choose its window using the recording’s drift timescale and event duration, and verify it does not remove sustained neural activity. Once the corrected baseline is demonstrably near zero, you can test `b=0` to hold it fixed. Don’t force zero merely because the input is called dF/F. This plot shows **long intervals of negative dF/F with almost no inferred spikes**, while spikes concentrate during positive excursions. That is consistent with a baseline problem, but **the red spike trace alone cannot tell us whether OASIS’s fitted baseline is wrong**.

The blue trace appears to shift its local baseline substantially. Your moving-mean subtraction could contribute: sustained activity pulls the moving mean upward, making nearby quieter periods negative.

I would test these changes **on this neuron first**:

1. **Plot `C + b` over the blue trace**, using the saved `C` and `b`. Also draw a horizontal line at `b`. This reveals whether OASIS is fitting the calcium waveform correctly. Red `S` should remain in a separate panel.

2. **Recompute dF/F without the moving-mean subtraction**, then rerun OASIS with your existing settings:
   ```python
   raw = allTraces[:, neuron_index].astype(float)
   F0 = np.percentile(raw, 25)
   y = raw / F0 - 1

   c, s, b, g, lam = deconvolve(
       y, penalty=1, b_nonneg=False
   )
   ```
   Compare the reconstruction and spike timing against the current result.

3. **If the unfiltered trace still drifts**, test a slowly varying lower-percentile baseline instead of a moving mean. Its window must be substantially longer than individual calcium events; too short a window will remove real activity. I would choose that window after seeing the unfiltered trace and knowing your frame rate.

For now, **keep `b_nonneg=False` and do not force `b=0`**. Your current preprocessing produces negative values, so requiring a nonnegative baseline could worsen the fit. OASIS estimates a scalar baseline per trace; it cannot use that parameter to follow the changing baseline visible here. [OASIS baseline options](https://github.com/j-friedrich/OASIS/blob/master/oasis/functions.py#L273-L279)

**The most useful next comparison is the same interval with unfiltered dF/F and `C+b`.** That will separate preprocessing distortion from an OASIS fitting issue.

Yes—**`c+b` follows the major rises and decays of the dF/F trace reasonably well**, while smoothing rapid fluctuations. This plot does not show an obvious baseline-fitting failure.

The earlier mismatch mainly came from comparing dF/F directly with `s`: spike estimates represent new activity driving the calcium signal, so they do not reproduce its baseline or prolonged decays.

**I would keep the current settings for now.** My earlier suggestion that baseline subtraction was causing the problem was a hypothesis; this reconstruction does not justify changing it.

A good reconstruction supports the fluorescence fit, but does not by itself validate spike timing or spike counts. Before applying changes broadly, check a few neurons and quieter intervals for similarly reasonable fits.

> how is the scale of spk compared to dff? If I have multiple sessions with different dff recording, deconvolved separately in each session, will the spk be comparable between them?

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check how OASIS preserves amplitude units and what session-specific fits mean for comparing your spike estimates.
>
> <details><summary>Searched the web</summary>
>
> Searched the web for `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/j-friedrich/OASIS/master/oasis/oasis_methods.pyx`
>
> </details>
</details>

**Your `spk` values retain the amplitude units of dF/F, but separately deconvolved sessions are not automatically calibrated to the same biological spike scale.** Separate deconvolution itself is fine; differences in normalization, recording quality, and fitted parameters matter.

For your AR(1) model:

\[
s_t=c_t-gc_{t-1}
\]

So `s` measures the new calcium-signal amplitude added at each frame. If the input uses fractional dF/F, `s=0.02` represents a **0.02 dF/F increment**, not 0.02 spikes. Its amplitude is not directly comparable to the full fluorescence peak, which includes accumulated activity from preceding frames. [OASIS model](https://github.com/j-friedrich/OASIS/blob/master/oasis/oasis_methods.pyx#L103-L118)

Across sessions, three things affect comparability:

| Source of variation | Effect |
|---|---|
| **Fluorescence normalization** | Your session-specific 25th-percentile F0 changes the denominator. Different activity distributions can change F0 even without a change in recording gain. |
| **Recording sensitivity and background** | dF/F cancels a purely multiplicative gain applied equally to signal and baseline. It does not generally correct changing background, ROI contamination, or fluorescence response per action potential. |
| **Deconvolution parameters** | Your calls estimate decay `g`, noise, and baseline separately. These influence inferred spike amplitudes and which small events survive the fit. [OASIS parameter estimation](https://github.com/j-friedrich/OASIS/blob/master/oasis/functions.py#L269-L304) |

For example, even with the **same fitted `c`**, different decay estimates change `s`:

```text
c[t−1] = 0.10, c[t] = 0.12

g = 0.90 → s[t] = 0.030
g = 0.98 → s[t] = 0.022
```

For your tracked-neuron, multi-session analysis, I suggest:

- **Use identical preprocessing and matched time bins across sessions.** Treat results as deconvolved dF/F activity, not firing rates in Hz.
- **Inspect each neuron’s F0, `g`, noise, and reconstruction quality across sessions.** Large changes warrant checking the underlying traces.
- **Consider a shared decay parameter per tracked neuron** if its kinetics appear stable across sessions. Estimate it from reliable recordings; do not force one value across all neurons merely to equalize amplitudes.
- **For TCA, avoid independently normalizing every session by its own maximum or standard deviation if session-to-session amplitude changes are important.** That would remove some of the changes you want to measure. A single normalization factor per neuron, applied across all its sessions, preserves those relative differences.

**Do not multiply by the saved `spikeScaleFactor` to “equalize” sessions.** That factor is F0: multiplication returns fluorescence amplitude units and can reintroduce recording-brightness differences. It does not supply the missing calibration from fluorescence to actual spike counts.

> decay is faster in tuning curve sessions and slower in behavioral sessions. could it be that behavioral sessions have more recurrent processing that harms decay estimate?

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check how OASIS estimates decay and whether sustained activity could make behavioral sessions appear to have slower calcium decay.
>
> <details><summary>Searched the web</summary>
>
> Searched the web for `site.journals.plos.org Friedrich Zhou Paninski 2017 deconvolution autocovariance decay time constants isolated events`
>
> </details>
</details>

**Yes—sustained or temporally correlated firing during behavior could make the estimated decay look slower.** Recurrent processing is one possible source of that activity, but the decay difference alone cannot establish that mechanism.

In your current call, OASIS estimates `g` from the fluorescence **autocovariance**. Persistent firing can extend fluorescence correlations, so the estimator can attribute some ongoing neural activity to a longer calcium decay. This is a plausible interpretation of your finding, based on [OASIS’s decay-estimation code](https://github.com/j-friedrich/OASIS/blob/master/oasis/functions.py#L1005-L1084).

The ambiguity is:

\[
\text{prolonged fluorescence}
\quad\longleftarrow\quad
\begin{cases}
\text{slow calcium decay}\\
\text{continued firing with faster calcium decay}
\end{cases}
\]

If tuning sessions have more isolated responses followed by quiet periods, they may provide cleaner estimates of calcium decay. **But tuning-derived decay is not automatically the true value**: overlapping responses, baseline drift, signal-to-noise differences, or calcium-response nonlinearities can also affect estimates.

This matters for your analysis. Since

\[
s_t=c_t-gc_{t-1},
\]

a larger `g`—slower decay—requires less new activity to explain the same positive calcium trace. An overestimated behavioral `g` could therefore suppress inferred sustained activity. **A good `c+b` reconstruction does not resolve that ambiguity.**

I would test it as follows:

1. **Compare decay in seconds**, using \(\tau=-1/[f_s\log(g)]\), rather than comparing `g` directly if frame rates differ.
2. **For each tracked neuron, inspect isolated event tails in both session types**, preferably with similar amplitudes and no visible subsequent events. Stimulus offset alone does not guarantee firing stopped.
3. **Rerun behavioral deconvolution using a fixed, independently estimated decay for that neuron**, while allowing baseline and noise to vary by session. Compare this with the freely estimated result.
4. **Check whether your scientific conclusions survive both choices.** A decay that is too fast can create artificial ongoing spikes along real calcium tails, so increased behavioral activity after fixing `g` is not itself proof of improvement.

My recommendation is to treat **shared decay per tracked neuron as a sensitivity analysis first**. If isolated-event estimates agree across session types but whole-session estimates differ, that would support activity-dependent bias in the whole-session estimate.

**This looks like a startup transient or baseline drift that your current correction has not removed.** The initial level rises from roughly −0.3 toward zero over several hundred frames. The plot alone cannot distinguish a recording artifact from real activity changes.

Your current calculation is effectively:

\[
dFF_{\mathrm{corrected}}(t)
=\frac{F(t)-\operatorname{movingMean}(F)(t)}{F_0}.
\]

The 1,000-frame moving mean includes later frames. If fluorescence rises near the start, the estimated baseline can exceed the early fluorescence, producing a negative dip. At the boundary, `mode='nearest'` also repeats the first sample. [SciPy filter behavior](https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.ndimage.uniform_filter1d.html)

**I would handle this in the following order:**

1. **Inspect raw `allTraces` over the same interval.** Plot several neurons and, if available, background fluorescence. A similar startup rise across many ROIs would support a recording-wide transient. Check registration and acquisition changes before treating it as neural activity.

2. **If those startup frames are unreliable, exclude them from fitting and analysis.** Deconvolve the stable segment, then place the result back into a full-length array with excluded frames marked `NaN`. **Do not shorten the stored array or shift behavioral frame indices.** Choose the cutoff from the raw recording quality, not solely to flatten this plot.

3. **If the frames are valid but the baseline drifts, test a baseline estimated from the lower envelope instead of the moving mean.** A rolling low percentile is one candidate, but its window must be long compared with calcium events and short enough to follow the drift. It can still mistake sustained neural activity for baseline, so validate it on representative traces.

For that alternative, keeping your existing session-level amplitude denominator gives:

```python
from scipy.ndimage import percentile_filter

F = allTraces.astype(float)
F0 = np.percentile(F, 25, axis=0)

# Example starting point for comparison, not a validated window.
baseline = percentile_filter(
    F, percentile=20, size=(1001, 1), mode="reflect"
)
dff = (F - baseline) / F0[None, :]
```

Here `size=(1001, 1)` filters along time without mixing neurons. [SciPy percentile filter](https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.ndimage.percentile_filter.html) Changing to `reflect` alone will not solve a genuine startup transient.

**For this particular example, I would inspect the raw startup frames first.** Don’t clip negative dF/F to zero or force OASIS’s baseline to zero; neither addresses the underlying drift. No code changed.

Then **check the moving-average subtraction first, rather than discarding the initial frames.** My earlier suggestion to exclude startup frames would not be appropriate if the raw recording is valid.

Your calculation has two stages:

```python
dff = rawTC / F0 - 1
dff_corrected = dff - moving_average(dff)
```

The first stage only rescales and shifts each neuron’s trace—it cannot introduce a new temporal shape. **The second stage can**, because its baseline depends on surrounding activity.

Run this comparison for the affected neuron:

```python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from scipy.ndimage import uniform_filter1d

F = allTraces[:, neuron_index].astype(float)
F0 = np.percentile(F, 25)

dff_plain = F / F0 - 1
baseline = uniform_filter1d(dff_plain, size=1000, mode="nearest")
dff_corrected = dff_plain - baseline

fig, ax = plt.subplots(3, 1, sharex=True)
ax[0].plot(F)
ax[0].set_ylabel("Raw F")

ax[1].plot(dff_plain, label="Plain dF/F")
ax[1].plot(baseline, label="Subtracted baseline")
ax[1].legend()

ax[2].plot(dff_corrected)
ax[2].set_ylabel("Corrected")
plt.show()
```

**If plain dF/F looks correct and only the corrected version has the dip, remove the moving-average subtraction for these data** and pass plain dF/F to OASIS with your existing `b_nonneg=False` setting. Don’t replace it with another detrending method unless the raw trace actually requires drift correction.

Also, your function computes a mean-centered variable named `baseline` but never uses it—the subtraction uses `baseline_smooth`. That is redundant code, although changing that variable would only change the constant offset, not fix the transient’s shape.

The first plot is **the 1,000-frame moving average of `allTraces(:,1)`, not the raw trace**:

```matlab
dffSmooth = smoothdata(allTraces(:,1),'movmean',1000);
```

It shows a gradual decline from about 835 to 780. That drift alone does not obviously explain the large negative startup dip you showed earlier.

**A specific possibility is an unusually high first frame.** Your Python filter uses `mode='nearest'`, which repeats that first value outside the recording boundary. A single high starting value can therefore inflate the moving baseline and create a negative dip lasting roughly half the filter window. MATLAB’s default shrinking endpoint window does not reproduce that padding behavior. [SciPy boundary handling](https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.ndimage.uniform_filter1d.html)

Check the **same neuron and session as the problematic dF/F**, especially the first few frames:

```matlab
F = double(allTraces(:,1));
F0 = prctile(F,25);

disp(F(1:10))
fprintf('F0 = %.3f; first frame = %.3f\n',F0,F(1));

% Reproduce the Python 1000-frame, nearest-padded moving mean.
w = 1000;
Fp = [repmat(F(1),w,1); F; repmat(F(end),w,1)];
Bp = movmean(Fp,w);
Bnearest = Bp(w + (1:numel(F)));

% Compare with a window that shrinks at the recording boundaries.
Bshrink = movmean(F,w,'Endpoints','shrink');

figure;
subplot(2,1,1)
plot(F); hold on
plot(Bnearest,'LineWidth',1.5);
plot(Bshrink,'LineWidth',1.5);
legend('Raw F','Nearest-padded mean','Shrinking-window mean');
xlim([1 min(1500,numel(F))]);

subplot(2,1,2)
plot((F-Bnearest)/F0); hold on
plot((F-Bshrink)/F0);
legend('Nearest-padded correction','Shrinking-window correction');
xlim([1 min(1500,numel(F))]);
```

**If the dip occurs only with nearest padding, fix the endpoint handling**, rather than deleting hundreds of valid frames or changing OASIS. If the first frame itself is corrupted, handle that acquisition artifact explicitly.

If both corrections show the dip, we should examine the unsmoothed trace and the estimated baseline together. The smoothed plot currently hides the information needed to distinguish these cases.

MATLAB’s `movmean` defaults to **shrinking the window at the endpoints**, averaging only available samples rather than repeating the first or last value. [MATLAB documentation](https://www.mathworks.com/help/matlab/ref/movmean.html)

Updated [deconvolve_TC.py](./pythonPackageRockfish/registration/step4_extractTC/deconvolve_TC.py) to use that behavior. Removed the redundant, unused baseline calculation too.

Verified both endpoint outliers and odd/even window sizes. This prevents boundary frames from being artificially repeated; **the outlier frame itself remains**. Frame counts, alignment, and F0 are unchanged.

Rerun without `--skip-existing` to regenerate dF/F and deconvolution outputs.

> in the deconvolution, the lamda values are fit right

Yes. In your current call:

```python
c, s, b, g, lam = deconvolve(trace, penalty=1, b_nonneg=False)
```

**OASIS determines `lam` separately for each neuron in each session**, using the estimated noise level to constrain the reconstruction error. You are not supplying a fixed lambda.

`lam` controls the sparsity penalty:

- Larger `lam` generally means stronger suppression of small inferred events.
- Smaller `lam` generally permits more inferred activity.

It is **not a spike-amplitude scaling factor**. Its value depends on signal units and noise, so differences between sessions do not directly indicate differences in neural activity.

Your script saves these fitted values as `lam` in each session’s `data_spkOps.mat`.

Implemented:

- [deconvolve_TC.py](./pythonPackageRockfish/registration/step4_extractTC/deconvolve_TC.py): adds `--session-folder imagingSessionDeconv` and a **2,000-frame moving 25th percentile**, shrinking at boundaries. The session-level F0 denominator remains unchanged.
- [deconvolve_TC_fixDecay.py](./pythonPackageRockfish/registration/step4_extractTC/deconvolve_TC_fixDecay.py): averages decay times per neuron, converts back to fixed `g`, and refits the other parameters.
- Added `deconvolve_TC_.py` as an alias and updated the statistics collector and Slurm scripts.

Run these in order from the script directory:

```powershell
python deconvolve_TC.py --base-dir B:\zz153_AC --session-folder imagingSessionDeconv

python collect_session_deconv_stats.py B:\zz153_AC --session-folder imagingSessionDeconv

python deconvolve_TC_fixDecay.py --base-dir B:\zz153_AC --session-folder imagingSessionDeconv
```

The fixed pass writes, within each session:

```text
data_S_fixed.mat
data_C_fixed.mat
data_spkOps_fixed.mat
```

It uses:
\[
\tau=-1/\log(g),\qquad g_{\mathrm{fixed}}=\exp(-1/\operatorname{mean}(\tau))
\]
where decay time is measured in frames. Add `--frame-rate 15` to also save seconds, if all sessions were recorded at 15 Hz. Invalid fits are excluded from averaging.

**Parallel batches remain supported.** Finish all first-pass workers, run the collector once, then launch the fixed-pass workers. Neuron columns and frame rates must correspond across sessions.

Requires **pandas** in addition to the existing dependencies. Four tests passed, including both passes with real OASIS on synthetic data. No experimental data was processed.

> is moving 25th percentile gonna be significantly slower than moving mean?

**Yes—the moving 25th percentile is substantially more expensive than a moving mean**, though its effect on total runtime depends on how long OASIS takes.

- **Moving mean:** updates a running sum, approximately \(O(T)\) per neuron.
- **Moving percentile:** maintains an ordered window, typically around \(O(T\log W)\), with larger overhead.

The new code uses pandas’ rolling quantile, which avoids explicitly sorting a fresh 2,000-frame window at every frame. It also avoids constructing a huge array of overlapping windows.

Your session-level parallelization still works. **I haven’t benchmarked representative recordings**, so I can’t give a reliable slowdown factor; the synthetic tests only verified correctness. Timing one real session would show whether baseline estimation or OASIS dominates.

> how do I run deconvolve_TC.py for the new folder?

From the directory containing `deconvolve_TC.py`, run:

```powershell
python deconvolve_TC.py --base-dir "B:\zz153_AC" --session-folder imagingSessionDeconv
```

This processes all sessions under `B:\zz153_AC\imagingSessionDeconv`.

For just the first alphabetically sorted session:

```powershell
python deconvolve_TC.py --base-dir "B:\zz153_AC" --session-folder imagingSessionDeconv --session 1
```

For your Slurm batch setup, from the animal directory with the scripts available:

```bash
sbatch deconvolve_TC_array.slurm --session-folder imagingSessionDeconv
```

Outputs are saved inside each session’s `suite2p/plane0` folder. Existing outputs are overwritten by default.

The main trial-extraction function is **[fn_parseTrialTC.m](./organizer/object_organzier/fn_parseTrialTC.m:1)**. The current pipeline is:

```text
Behavior MAT: allData ──→ Animal.getBehav ──→ sessionInfo.beh / behSel
Session TC ──────────────────────────────→ Animal.parseTrial
                                                  ↓
                                          fn_parseTrialTC
                                                  ↓
                                  dffStim / dffChoice / dffReward
```

**1. Match behavior to each recording session**

[Animal.m — getBehav, line 169](./organizer/object_organzier/Animal.m:169) loads:

```matlab
<behavPath>/matlab/<mouse>_*_2AFCsession*.mat
```

It reads `allData`, removes entirely NaN rows, and matches the recording by **date and session name**. Each remaining row represents a trial:

| `allData` column | Meaning used by the code |
|---|---|
| 2–4 | Stimulus, action, response type |
| 5–6 | Stimulus and response times |
| 7 | Stimulus imaging-frame index |
| 8 | Response imaging-frame index |
| 9 | Reward imaging-frame index |

**2. Extract trial TC**

[Animal.m — parseTrial, line 260](./organizer/object_organzier/Animal.m:260) transposes the session TC from **frames × neurons** to **neurons × frames**, then calls:

```matlab
F = sessionInfo.TC{j}';
beh = sessionInfo.beh{j};
[dffStim,dffChoice,dffReward] = fn_parseTrialTC(F,beh);
```

Inside `fn_parseTrialTC`, each trial ends **one frame before the next trial’s stimulus**, or at recording end for the last trial:

```matlab
if i < nTrials
    endFrame = behData(i+1,7)-1;
else
    endFrame = size(F,2);
end

stimFrameStart = behData(i,7)-30;
choiceFrame    = behData(i,8)-30;
rewardFrame    = choiceFrame+3;

stimTC   = F(:,stimFrameStart:endFrame);
choiceTC = F(:,choiceFrame:endFrame);
rewardTC = F(:,rewardFrame:endFrame);
```

Outputs are **neurons × time × trials**, padded with NaNs to equal length. `Animal.parseTrial` then keeps at most the first **100 frames** and stores them in `sessionInfo.dffStim`, `dffChoice`, and `dffReward`. Despite these names, they contain spikes when the input TC is spikes.

**Important details:**

- Stimulus/choice alignment places the event at **sample 31**, because extraction starts 30 frames before it.
- **Reward extraction does not use column 9.** It assumes reward occurs three frames after the response.
- This path uses frame indices already stored in `allData`; it does not derive them from timestamps.
- **I did not find the behavioral `.txt` → `allData` conversion in this repository.** The current Animal pipeline starts with the processed behavioral `.mat` file.

No files changed.

Implemented [fn_selectNeuralTrials.m](./analysis/trialSelection/fn_selectNeuralTrials.m), with a [ready-to-edit example](./analysis/trialSelection/example_selectNeuralTrials.m).

It supports all six requirements and returns:

- `trialInfo`: selected trials, animal labels, and original session/trial indices.
- `neuralActivity`: neurons × frames × trials, optionally separate cells per animal.
- `tracking`: per-trial `ishere` flags and neurons tracked across all selected sessions within each animal.
- `selection`: RT matching diagnostics, trial/neuron tradeoff, and recommended count.

RT matching uses equal histogram counts across animals within each period/type. Automatic selection uses a documented greedy heuristic; you can override the trial count.

**Validation:** 11 tests passed, plus checks on real zz172/zz159 files. For the tested filters, automatic selection chose **2 trials per group**, retaining **595 and 819 tracked neurons**, respectively. [Saved results](B:/analysis/trialSelection_validation/selected_trials.mat).

![Trial versus tracked-neuron tradeoff](B:/analysis/trialSelection_validation/tradeoff.png)

> well, one session should have 60-100 trials, so why only select 1 trial? you should have at least good tracking for 100 trials;

You’re right. **Tracking is constant within a session**; selecting more trials from that session should not reduce the tracked-neuron count.

My original balancing rule was too restrictive: the rarest condition capped every other condition. I’ve [fixed the selector](./analysis/trialSelection/fn_selectNeuralTrials.m) to:

- Match animals within each condition without equalizing different conditions.
- Require **at least 100 trials per animal** by default.
- Plot total trials per animal.

With the same RT/type/period filters, it now selects **142 trials per animal**, retaining **136 neurons across zz172’s selected sessions** and **673 across zz159’s**. All 14 tests passed.

![Corrected trial–neuron tradeoff](B:/analysis/trialSelection_validation/tradeoff.png)

> delete the part in fn_selectNeuralTrials.m about tracking ROI. simply select all the trials that meets the criteria; I can look at neurons that are selected later

> function [trialInfo, neuralActivity, selection] = fn_selectNeuralTrials(sources, varargin)
> %FN_SELECTNEURALTRIALS Return every parsed trial that satisfies the filters.
> % [trialInfo, activity, selection] = fn_selectNeuralTrials(files,...)
> % files: MAT filename(s) containing sessionInfo, or a table/cell of tables.
> %
> % Options:
> % AnimalLabels       One unique label per source; default file stems.
> % RT                 Inclusive RT range in seconds (default [0 Inf]).
> % TrialTypes         Table with label, stimulus, action columns (AND within
> %                    each row, OR across rows). Alternatively, label and
> %                    predicate columns, with predicates in cells: @(beh) mask.
> %                    Empty means all types. Overlapping types keep each trial
> %                    once; matching labels are joined with " | ".
> % LearningPeriods    dayLabel values ([] means all, including unlabeled).
> % ActivityField      Parsed field to read ('dffStim' by default).
> % FrameIndices       Frame indices ([] keeps all; shorter traces NaN padded).
> % ExcludeFlaggedSessions Skip excludeNeuralAnalysis==true sessions (true).
> % OutputFormat       'pooled' (default): neurons x frames x trials tensor.
> %                    'cell': one compact tensor per animal.
> %
> % There is no balancing, downsampling, trial quota, or neuron selection.
> % The RT range is applied independently to each animal. Files are loaded one
> % at a time. Every activity row is preserved, in single precision, without
> % additional masking or normalization. Source files are never modified.
> %
> % trialInfo preserves behSel columns and adds animal, animalIndex, sourceFile,
> % sessionRow (original sessionInfo row), trialInSession (original behSel row),
> % dayLabel, trialType, trialIndex, and activityTrial (index within animal).
> % Session date/name/number, sessionRec, day, sessionType and taskLabel are
> % copied when available. Order is source, then session, then original trial.
> %
> % Pooled: trialInfo(k,:) describes activity(:,:,k). Each animal has a separate
> % neuron block; other animals' blocks are NaN. selection.animals gives the
> % pooledNeuronStart/End and original neuron counts for these blocks.
> % Cell: trialInfo(trialInfo.animalIndex==a,:) describes activity{a}, whose
> % neuron rows are the original rows in that animal's parsed activity.
> % Different animals may have different counts or no matching trials.
> % An error is raised only if there are no matching recorded trials overall.

Updated [fn_selectNeuralTrials.m](./analysis/trialSelection/fn_selectNeuralTrials.m).

- Returns **all trials meeting the filters**, with no balancing or downsampling.
- Preserves every neural activity row without tracking-based masking.
- Copies the original session `ishere` unchanged into **`trialInfo.ishere`** for each trial.
- Removes tracking optimization and trial-count thresholds.

> function trialTypeInfo = fn_selectNeuralTrials(files, varargin)
> %FN_SELECTNEURALTRIALS Select saved trialInfo without loading neural data.
> % trialTypeInfo = fn_selectNeuralTrials(files,...)
> % Output: nAnimal x nStage x nTrialType cell array of tables.
> % Axis order follows files, LearningPeriods, and TrialTypes rows, respectively.
> % Empty combinations contain zero-row tables. Every matching row is retained;
> % overlapping trial types are evaluated independently in their own cells.
> %
> % Only load(file,'trialInfo') is used. Original columns (including ishere if
> % saved in trialInfo) are preserved. No sessionInfo or neural arrays are read.
> % Adds animal, animalIndex, sourceFile, sourceTrialRow, and trialType.
> %
> % Options:
> % AnimalLabels    One unique label per file (default filename stems).
> % RT              Inclusive range in seconds ([0 Inf]); excludes nonfinite RT.
> % LearningPeriods Ordered dayLabel values ([] discovers their stable union).

> %                 Empty means a single type named "all".

> now I want to write a separate function to look at tracking over the trial selected.
> imagine a function that only takes in one entry in 'trialTypeInfo', or, i.e. trials table. and organize all the trials back to the original sessionInfo by session; for each session, load its offsetMap. among all trials, create an average offsetMap across all the sessions where they has trials selected, by weighted by how many trials it has. then, for each session, make an 'offset score' which records the offset is has with the reference average offset, but again weighted by how many trials it has. Finally, give out a curve to have 'sessions taken' and neurons tracked in a figure. finally, the experimenter can now decide how many session to take to have a good amount of tracking.
> this function will be convenient becuase if I want neurons tracked for multiple learnign period, or trial types, I can just concatenate these tables to get a new list of tracked neurons.

<details><summary>15 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check how `offsetMap` and `ishere` are stored, then add a function that takes a trials table, ranks its sessions against a trial-weighted reference offset map, and plots the neurons tracked as sessions are added.
>
> <details><summary>Ran 2 commands</summary>
>
> - Ran `Get-Content -LiteralPath 'analysis\trialSelection\fn_selectNeuralTrials.m' -TotalCount 140; Get-ChildItem -LiteralPath 'B:\analysis\animal' -Filter '*.mat' | Select-Object Name,Length; git -c safe.directory=C:/Users/zzhu34/Documents/GitHub/analyzeMeso status --short`
> - Ran `rg --files -g AGENTS.md; Get-Content -LiteralPath 'analysis\trialSelection\fn_loadSelectedNeuralActivity.m'; rg -n "offsetMap|avgOffset|ishereMat|chooseBestCandidate" organizer/object_organzier/Animal.m preprocess/roiTracking -g '*.m'`
>
> </details>
>
> I’ll use:
>
> - **Reference map:** the mean offset map, weighted by selected trial counts.
> - **Offset score:** each session’s mean absolute difference from that reference, multiplied by its fraction of selected trials.
> - **Curve:** add sessions from lowest score upward and count neurons with `ishere == 1` in every included session.
>
> Concatenated tables must come from the same animal/file. Duplicate trials will count once, and sessions missing offset maps or tracking flags will be reported separately.
>
> <details><summary>Created a file, edited a file, ran 3 commands</summary>
>
> - Ran `Get-Content -LiteralPath 'preprocess\roiTracking\main\step7_evaluateTracking.m' | Select-Object -Skip 113 -First 30; Get-Content -LiteralPath 'organizer\object_organzier\Animal.m' | Select-Object -Skip 1380 -First 40; Get-Content -LiteralPath 'analysis\trialSelection\example_selectNeuralTrials.m'`
> - Ran `@'
> function tracking = fn_evaluateTrialTracking(trials, varargin)
> %FN_EVALUATETRIALTRACKING Rank sessions and inspect cumulative ROI tracking.
> % tracking = fn_evaluateTrialTracking(trialTypeInfo{animal,stage,type})
> % tracking = fn_evaluateTrialTracking([table1; table2; ...])
> % Tables must belong to ONE source file/animal. Source is read from sourceFile.
> % Concatenating learning periods/types is supported; duplicate original trials
> % (same session and trialInSession) count once in the weights and curve.
> %
> % For eligible sessions with n(s) unique selected trials:
> %   reference(p) = sum_s n(s)*offsetMap_s(p) / sum_s n(s)
> % where sums at each pixel omit nonfinite map values.
> %   meanAbsoluteOffset(s) = mean(abs(offsetMap_s - reference), finite pixels)
> %   trialWeight(s) = n(s)/sum_s n(s)
> %   offsetScore(s) = trialWeight(s)*meanAbsoluteOffset(s)
> % Scores use the ORIGINAL full eligible set; they are not recalculated when
> % sessions are added. Sessions are ranked by increasing offsetScore; ties
> % prefer more selected trials, then the original sessionInfo row.
> % This ranks weighted offset mismatch contributions. It can place a small
> % session before a larger one with the same mismatch. It is NOT an optimizer
> % for neuron count. The curve measures actual intersections of ishere==1.
> %
> % Only optional parameter: 'Plot', true (default) or false.
> % No input/source files are modified and no trials are automatically removed.
> %
> % Outputs:
> % .sessions             Ranked session metadata, original offsetMap/ishere,
> %                       trial counts, weights, deviations and offset scores.
> % .referenceOffsetMap   Trial-weighted reference map (original map units).
> % .curve                nSessions, nTrials, nTrackedNeurons for each prefix.
> % .trackedNeurons       nOriginalNeurons x nSessions logical matrix; column k
> %                       flags neurons present in ALL first k ranked sessions.
> % .trialSessionRank     Rank for each INPUT table row; NaN for excluded sessions.
> % .uniqueTrialMask      First occurrence of each original trial in the input.
> % .excludedSessions     Missing/invalid-map or missing-ishere sessions.
> % .figure               Figure handle ([] when Plot=false).
> %
> % To take the first k sessions after inspecting the plot:
> %   keep = tracking.uniqueTrialMask & tracking.trialSessionRank <= k;
> %   chosenTrials = trials(keep,:);
> %   neurons = find(tracking.trackedNeurons(:,k)); % Original ROI row indices.
> %
> % Sessions with no finite offset map or an empty ishere are excluded and
> % reported. Zero-valued ishere vectors are VALID and yield zero tracked ROIs.
> % Incompatible map shapes or ROI counts raise errors rather than guessing.
>
> p = inputParser;
> addParameter(p,'Plot',true,@(x)islogical(x)&&isscalar(x));
> parse(p,varargin{:});
> assert(istable(trials)&&height(trials)>0,'evaluateTrialTracking:Input', ...

> function tracking = fn_evaluateTrialTracking(trials, opsFile, varargin)
> %FN_EVALUATETRIALTRACKING Rank sessions using ops.mat, without parsed activity.
> % tracking = fn_evaluateTrialTracking(trials,'B:\zz172_PPC2\ops.mat')
> % Concatenate trialTypeInfo tables from ONE animal to combine stages/types.
> % Duplicate original trials count once. No parsedTrial.mat is opened.
> %
> % Offset maps come from ops.offsetMap. Session date/name are matched to
> % ops.imagingFilename, independently of trial table order. If imagingFilename
> % is absent, sessionRec must index the original recording order in ops.
> %
> % Presence flags come from trials.ishere when supplied. Otherwise loads only
> % ishereFinal (sessions x neurons) from stackROI_final_tracked.mat beside ops.
> % 'TrackingFile',path overrides that fallback. 'Plot',false suppresses plots.
> %
> % For eligible sessions with n(s) unique selected trials:
> %   reference(p) = sum_s n(s)*offsetMap_s(p) / sum_s n(s)
> % where sums at each pixel omit nonfinite map values.
> %   meanAbsoluteOffset(s) = mean(abs(offsetMap_s-reference), finite pixels)
> %   trialWeight(s) = n(s)/sum_s n(s)
> %   offsetScore(s) = trialWeight(s)*meanAbsoluteOffset(s)
> % Rank by increasing score; ties prefer more trials, then ops session index.
> % Reference and weights are fixed across the whole curve, not recalculated.
> % The score is weighted mismatch, so small sessions can rank before large
> % sessions with similar offsets. Actual tracking uses intersections of ishere==1.
> %
> % .sessions: ranked metadata, opsSessionIndex, original maps/flags, counts,
> %            trialWeight, meanAbsoluteOffset and offsetScore.
> % .referenceOffsetMap: trial-weighted average in original map units.
> % .curve: nSessions, nTrials, nTrackedNeurons for every ranked prefix.
> % .trackedNeurons: nOriginalNeurons x nSessions; column k gives common ROIs.
> % .trialSessionRank: rank for each INPUT row (NaN for excluded sessions).
> % .uniqueTrialMask: first occurrence of each original trial in the input.
> % .excludedSessions: unmatched sessions or missing maps/flags, with reasons.
> % .figure: plot handle, or [].
> %
> % Choose k after inspecting the curve:
> %   keep = tracking.uniqueTrialMask & tracking.trialSessionRank<=k;
> %   chosenTrials = trials(keep,:);
> %   neuronIDs = find(tracking.trackedNeurons(:,k));
> % No source data or input trials are modified.
>
> p = inputParser;
> addParameter(p,'Plot',true,@(x)islogical(x)&&isscalar(x));
> addParameter(p,'TrackingFile','');
> parse(p,varargin{:});
> assert(istable(trials)&&height(trials)>0,'evaluateTrialTracking:Input', ...
>     'Provide a nonempty trials table, e.g. trialTypeInfo{1,1,1}.');
> assert(ismember('trialInSession',trials.Properties.VariableNames), ...
>     'evaluateTrialTracking:Columns','Trials must contain trialInSession.');
> validateattributes(trials.trialInSession,{'numeric'},{'column','integer','positive','finite'});
> sources = repmat("",height(trials),1);
> if ismember('sourceFile',trials.Properties.VariableNames)
>     sources = string(trials.sourceFile);
>     assert(numel(unique(lower(replace(sources,'/','\'))))==1, ...
>         'evaluateTrialTracking:MixedAnimals','Concatenate stages/types from one animal/file only.');
> end
> if ismember('animal',trials.Properties.VariableNames)
>     assert(numel(unique(string(trials.animal)))==1,'evaluateTrialTracking:MixedAnimals', ...
>         'Concatenate stages/types from one animal only.');
> end
> d = load(char(opsFile),'ops');
> assert(isfield(d,'ops')&&isstruct(d.ops)&&isfield(d.ops,'offsetMap'), ...
>     'evaluateTrialTracking:Ops','opsFile must contain ops.offsetMap.');

> if isfield(ops,'mouseArea') && ~ismissing(sources(1)) && sources(1)~=""

>     assert(strcmpi(erase(string(stem),'_parsedTrial'),string(ops.mouseArea)), ...
>         'evaluateTrialTracking:MixedAnimals','The trials source and ops.mouseArea identify different datasets.');
> end
> mapsAll = ops.offsetMap;
> if iscell(mapsAll), nMap = numel(mapsAll);
> else
>     assert(isnumeric(mapsAll)&&isreal(mapsAll)&&ndims(mapsAll)<=3, ...
>         'evaluateTrialTracking:Map','ops.offsetMap must be rows x columns x sessions, or a cell vector.');
>     nMap = size(mapsAll,3);
> end
> hasDates = all(ismember({'date','sessionName'},trials.Properties.VariableNames));
> if hasDates, keys = trials(:,{'date','sessionName'});
> else
>     assert(ismember('sessionRec',trials.Properties.VariableNames), ...

>         'evaluateTrialTracking:Keys','Without date/sessionName, sessionRec must identify recorded sessions.');

Added [fn_applyToFiles.m](./helper_function/fn_applyToFiles.m). Your function receives each file’s full path.

```matlab
% Only .mat files in this folder
fn_applyToFiles('B:\analysis\animal', @myFunction, ...
    'Extensions', '.mat');

% Include subfolders and multiple extensions
fn_applyToFiles('B:\analysis', @myFunction, ...
    'Recursive', true, 'Extensions', {'.mat', '.h5'});

% Pass additional arguments to your function
files = fn_applyToFiles('B:\analysis\animal', ...
    @(file) myFunction(file, extraArg), 'Extensions', '.mat');
```

Defaults: all extensions, no subfolders. Returns processed paths; stops if your function errors.

> where in the tracking structure which saves the identity of sessions that is selected

`tracking.sessions` contains the session identities, **ordered by offset score**. Its columns include:

- `date` and `sessionName`: session identity, when present in the input.
- `opsSessionIndex`: index of that session in `ops.offsetMap`.
- `sessionRec`: original recording index, when available.
- `rank`: order in which sessions enter the curve.

The function ranks sessions; you choose how many to keep. For example:

```matlab
k = 5;
selectedSessions = tracking.sessions(1:k,:);

% Get the corresponding input trials
keep = tracking.uniqueTrialMask & tracking.trialSessionRank <= k;
selectedTrials = trials(keep,:);
```

`tracking.trialSessionRank` maps each input trial to its session’s rank; excluded sessions have `NaN`.

Previously, `offsetScore = meanAbsoluteOffset`, so larger sessions received no explicit preference. I’ve now implemented your logarithmic weighting in [fn_evaluateTrialTracking.m](./analysis/trialSelection/fn_evaluateTrialTracking.m).

For session \(s\), let \(n_s\) be its unique selected trial count and \(M_s(p)\) its offset map.

**1. Trial-weighted reference map**
\[
R(p)=\frac{\sum_s n_s M_s(p)}{\sum_s n_s}
\]
Nonfinite map values are omitted pixel by pixel.

**2. Mean absolute offset error**
\[
D_s=\operatorname{mean}_{p}\left|M_s(p)-R(p)\right|
\]

**3. Final ranking score**
\[
P_s=100\frac{n_s}{\sum_j n_j},
\qquad
\boxed{\mathrm{score}_s=\frac{D_s}{\ln(1+P_s)}}
\]

Percentages use eligible sessions and stay fixed throughout the curve. Sessions are sorted by **lowest score first**.

Your example, assuming equal offset errors of 1:

| Selected trial percentage | Score |
|---|---:|
| 1% | \(1/\ln(2)=1.443\) |
| 10% | \(1/\ln(11)=0.417\) |

The 10% session ranks earlier. The exact MATLAB calculation is:

```matlab
sessions.trialPercent = 100*sessions.nTrials/sum(sessions.nTrials);
sessions.scoreDivisor = log1p(sessions.trialPercent);
sessions.offsetScore = sessions.meanAbsoluteOffset./sessions.scoreDivisor;
```

These columns are saved in `tracking.sessions`. Rerun the function to regenerate the graph. All 11 tests passed.

Added [fn_evaluateTrialTrackingBalanced.m](./analysis/trialSelection/fn_evaluateTrialTrackingBalanced.m). Your existing function is unchanged.

```matlab
trials = cat(1,trialTypeInfo{1,:,1});
opsFile = 'B:\zz172_PPC2\ops.mat';
phases = ["T1E","T1M","T1L"];

trackingError = fn_evaluateTrialTrackingBalanced(trials,opsFile, ...
    'PhaseOrder',phases,'Method','error');

trackingGreedy = fn_evaluateTrialTrackingBalanced(trials,opsFile, ...
    'PhaseOrder',phases,'Method','greedy');
```

Both methods cycle **T1E → T1M → T1L → T1E → …**, skipping exhausted phases.

- **`error`**: selects the session with lowest mean squared error from the pixelwise median reference map. Each session contributes equally.
- **`greedy`**: selects the session retaining the most neurons shared across **all previously selected sessions**. The first choice maximizes available neurons; ties use squared error.

The output retains `sessions`, `trackedNeurons`, and `trialSessionRank`. `curve.nSessionsByPhase` reports cumulative session counts for each phase. This balances session counts, not trial counts.

Added a [comparison example](./analysis/trialSelection/example_evaluateTrialTrackingBalanced.m) that overlays both methods. All eight new MATLAB tests passed.

> why do we need trials as input

`trials` defines **which sessions to consider** and provides their learning phases (`dayLabel`). It also lets the function map the ranked sessions back to your selected trials and report retained trial counts.

The current ranking itself does **not** use trial counts. It needs only:

- Session identities mapped to `ops.offsetMap`
- Learning phase for each session
- `ishere` flags for neuron tracking

So a session table—with one row per session—would be sufficient if you only want session ranking and tracking curves. The trial table is useful because it connects this step directly to your earlier trial selection.

Updated [fn_evaluateTrialTrackingBalanced.m](./analysis/trialSelection/fn_evaluateTrialTrackingBalanced.m):

```matlab
tracking = fn_evaluateTrialTrackingBalanced(trials,opsFile, ...
    'PhaseOrder',["T1E","T1M","T1L"], ...
    'MinSessionsPerStage',3, ...
    'MatchRemaining',false, ...
    'Method','greedy');
```

- `MinSessionsPerStage`: defaults to **3**; also accepts one minimum per stage.
- `MatchRemaining=true`: continues cycling through stages after the minimums.
- `MatchRemaining=false`: afterward, chooses from **any stage**.
- `Method='greedy'`: automatically picks the session preserving the most neurons shared across all previously selected sessions. No interaction required.

The function ranks all eligible sessions. `tracking.minimumPrefixLength` identifies the prefix meeting achievable minimums; `tracking.minimumStatus` reports shortages.

For unrestricted greedy selection from the start, set the minimum to `0` and `MatchRemaining=false`.

All 11 MATLAB tests passed.


> what sessions are excluded

`tracking.excludedSessions` lists the excluded sessions and their reasons:

```matlab
disp(tracking.excludedSessions)
```

A session is excluded when:

- **No matching ops recording:** its date/session name cannot be matched, or its `sessionRec` index is invalid.
- **Missing or invalid offsetMap:** the map is empty, invalid, or contains no finite values.
- **Missing ishere:** its neuron-presence vector is empty. An all-zero vector is valid and is **not** excluded.

The balanced function uses these same exclusions. Stage minimums and ranking do **not** exclude sessions; every eligible session appears in `tracking.sessions`.

Sessions absent from your input `trials` table are never considered, so they won’t appear in either output table.

Added [fn_loadTrackedNeuralActivity.m](./analysis/trialSelection/fn_loadTrackedNeuralActivity.m) and replaced `funName` in your TCA script.

```matlab
[a, neuralTrialInfo, neuronInfo] = fn_loadTrackedNeuralActivity( ...
    sessionInfoFile, tracking, trials, phases, types, ...
    'NumSessions', k, 'ActivityField', 'dffStim');
```

- `k`: your chosen number of ranked sessions.
- `a{phase,type}`: **neurons × frames × trials**.
- `neuralTrialInfo{phase,type}`: corresponding trial rows.
- `neuronInfo.neuronIDs`: original neuron indices.

It matches `sessionRec` identifiers to `sessionInfo`, then uses `trialInSession` to retrieve activity.

By default, it returns neurons tracked across **all chosen sessions**. Use `'NeuronSelection','all'` to retain every neuron.

For arbitrary sessions, pass `tracking.sessions(chosenRows,:)` instead of `tracking` and omit `NumSessions`.

`sessionInfoFile` must contain neural `sessionInfo`, such as `*_parsedTrial.mat`. Eight MATLAB tests passed.

> this is TC table, compute zscore of each neuron before averaging the trials or after

> this is TC table, compute zscore of each neuron before averaging the trials or after
>
> User context
>
> Images:
> - `~/AppData/Local/Temp/codex-clipboard-90a4e27d-9e58-441e-86d2-572061111ab0.png`

For comparing neurons across learning phases and trial types, **z-score before averaging trials**, using **the same mean and standard deviation for each neuron across all TC cells**.

Your cells are `neurons × time × trials`. For example:

```matlab
% Estimate one mean and SD per neuron across all conditions/time/trials.
pooled = cat(3, TC{:});
pooled = reshape(pooled, size(pooled,1), []);

mu = mean(pooled, 2, 'omitnan');
sd = std(pooled, 0, 2, 'omitnan');
sd(sd == 0) = 1;

% Normalize, then average trials.
TCmeanZ = cellfun(@(x) mean((x-mu)./sd, 3, 'omitnan'), ...
    TC, 'UniformOutput', false);
```

Each `TCmeanZ{phase,type}` is now **620 × 100**.

Avoid estimating a separate z-score from each condition’s averaged trace: that rescales every condition independently and removes differences in response magnitude.

With fixed `mu` and `sd`, normalizing before or after averaging gives the same result. The important distinction is **where you estimate those normalization parameters**.

> change this code to (1) do normalization based on squared activity; and (2), do the normalization by pooling all trial types and all time points together, then applying to each neuron

Use **RMS normalization**: divide each neuron’s activity by the square root of its mean squared activity, pooled across **all TC cells, time points, and trials**.

```matlab
% Pool all learning phases, trial types, time points, and trials.
pooled = cat(3, TC{:});
pooled = reshape(pooled, size(pooled,1), []);

% One normalization factor per neuron.
scale = sqrt(mean(pooled.^2, 2, 'omitnan'));
scale(scale == 0) = 1;

% Apply the same factor to that neuron in every condition.
TCnorm = cellfun(@(x) x ./ scale, ...
    TC, 'UniformOutput', false);

% Average trials after normalization.
TCmeanNorm = cellfun(@(x) mean(x, 3, 'omitnan'), ...
    TCnorm, 'UniformOutput', false);
```

Each `TCmeanNorm{phase,type}` is **neurons × time**. Squaring is used only to calculate the normalization factor; the output preserves the activity’s sign and does not subtract its mean.

Yes—the spike normalization explains why `scale` clusters near **0.01**.

`normSpk` is enabled for [zz153_PPC](./organizer/ops_param/zz153_PPC_param.m:34). In [Animal.m](./organizer/object_organzier/Animal.m:1325), each neuron is divided by:

\[
D_j=\sum_s \frac{N_s}{\sum_s N_s}\sqrt{\sum_t x_{s,t,j}^{\,2}}
\]

This is a recording-length-weighted **L2 norm**, rather than RMS. The same divisor is applied across all sessions for that neuron.

For zz153’s recording lengths, assuming similar raw RMS across recordings, this predicts a normalized RMS of **0.01015**—close to your histogram. Differences arise because your `TC` contains selected trial windows.

**Your new RMS normalization still works correctly.** The earlier positive scaling cancels:

\[
\frac{x/D_j}{\operatorname{RMS}(x/D_j)}
=
\frac{x}{\operatorname{RMS}(x)}
\]

Thus, `scale ≈ 0.01` is expected; the pooled **single-trial activity after division** should have RMS approximately 1.

One additional issue: [fn_selectTrialTCA_perAni](./analysis/TCA/trialSelection/fn_selectTrialTCA_perAni.m:65) computes `TCnorm`, but currently returns the original `TC`. To return normalized single-trial activity, add before the final `end`:

```matlab
TC = TCnorm;
```

> split each entry into 10 equal amount of trials on 3rd dimension, giv eme code for that in matlab
>
> User context
>
> Images:
> - `~/AppData/Local/Temp/codex-clipboard-e94d7fb0-6abd-4062-a36a-67aa8aab509f.png`

This creates a **10 × 2 cell array**: rows are trial groups, columns correspond to the original `TC` entries. Trial order is preserved.

```matlab
nGroups = 10;
TCsplit = cell(nGroups, numel(TC));
TCleftover = cell(size(TC));

for c = 1:numel(TC)
    x = TC{c};
    nPerGroup = floor(size(x,3) / nGroups);
    assert(nPerGroup >= 1, 'Each entry needs at least 10 trials.');

    for g = 1:nGroups
        idx = (g-1)*nPerGroup + (1:nPerGroup);
        TCsplit{g,c} = x(:,:,idx);
    end

    TCleftover{c} = x(:,:,nGroups*nPerGroup+1:end);
end
```

For your data:

- **212 trials:** 10 groups of 21; 2 leftover.
- **376 trials:** 10 groups of 37; 6 leftover.

The leftovers are retained separately because exactly equal groups cannot include all trials here.

The file **`pdf.worker.min.mjs`** is usually a support file for **PDF.js**, a JavaScript library used to display PDFs in browsers and apps. Mozilla’s source code produces that exact filename. [Source](https://github.com/mozilla/pdf.js/blob/master/gulpfile.mjs)

- **`.mjs`**: a JavaScript module.
- **`worker`**: runs PDF processing in the background.
- **`min`**: code compacted to reduce file size.

> The code never falls back to opening parsedTrial.mat during metadata selection.
> The existing 12 condition definitions, RT filter, correct/miss filters remain.
> Default RT filtering is ON, [0.35 0.65]; set cfg.useRTFilter=false to disable.
> One animal/area per call: different animals do not share neuron identities.
>
> 2. CHOOSE THE SESSION / TRACKED-CELL TRADEOFF
>
> Inspect curated.tracking.curve and its two-panel figure:
>   nSessions: size of each ranked prefix
>   nTrials: unique selected trials across all conditions in that prefix
>   nTrackedNeurons: intersection of ishere==1 across that prefix
>   nSessionsByPhase: counts in the order given by tracking.phaseOrder
>
> curated = chooseRSASessions(curated,12);
>
> This changes the retained prefix without reloading metadata or recomputing the
> ranking. It can be called again with a larger or smaller count, including 0.
> Inf keeps every eligible session. Finite counts above availability keep all.
> The red line on the tracking curve marks the current cutoff.
>
> curated.selection(k).trials               retained trials for condition k
> curated.selectionQC                      before RT / after RT / retained counts
> curated.sessionExcludedTrials            condition-level exclusions and reasons
> curated.tracking.sessions                ALL eligible sessions in ranked order
> curated.tracking.selectedSessions        chosen session rows, including sessionRec
> curated.tracking.selectedSessionRec      original recording identifiers
> curated.tracking.selectedNeuronIDs       common neuron indices for chosen sessions
> curated.tracking.selectedNeuronMask      logical mask in original neuron order
> curated.tracking.selectedMinimumMet      whether chosen prefix meets stage quotas
> curated.tracking.minimumStatus           requested / available / shortfall by phase
>
> Session scoring and balance:
>   reference(p) = median_s(offsetMap_s(p)), equal weight for each eligible session.
>   error(s) = mean_p((offsetMap_s(p)-reference(p))^2), finite pixels only.
>   'error': choose the lowest error among eligible candidates.
>   'greedy': maximize the intersection of ishere==1 with ALL previously selected
>             sessions. The first session maximizes its own present cell count.
>             Ties use lower error, then metadata row index.
> There is no trial-count weighting. The reference is fixed over the full curve.
> This greedy ordering is a heuristic, not an optimal search over all subsets.
>
> cfg.phaseOrder controls learning-stage rounds; [] uses first appearance among
> eligible selected trials. Labels are dayLabel values. By default choose 3
> sessions per stage, skipping unavailable stages, then continue phase rounds.
> Set cfg.matchRemaining=false to select from all remaining stages after quotas.
> An explicit cfg.phaseOrder can include absent stages to report their shortage.
> The quota does NOT set a final stopping point. cfg.numSessions/chooseRSASessions
> sets that point; a smaller prefix can cut off before quotas are met.
>
> Session eligibility uses the trials actually selected by the condition and RT
> filters. Behavior-only sessions, unmatched sessionRec, invalid trial indices,
> and (when requireGoodTracking=true) bad goodTracking flags are excluded.
> Tracking also excludes missing/invalid offset maps and empty ishere vectors.
> Zero ishere flags are valid absent neurons. Present means exactly ishere==1.
> Existing excludeNeuralAnalysis is not an additional filter in this pipeline.
> Rows are joined by sessionRec, never by table position. Shared date/sessionName/
> dayLabel columns are checked for consistency. Behavioral sessions already split
> from daily registrations use their saved per-session maps and flags directly.
>
> cfg.selectSessionsByTracking=false disables ranking and map/ishere requirements;
> metadata mapping and cfg.requireGoodTracking still apply. Use numSessions=Inf.
> An entirely empty eligible selection returns empty tracking and zero counts.
>
> 3. LOAD ACTIVITY ONLY WHEN READY
>
> neuralCurated = loadCuratedNeuralData(curated,[base '.mat']);
>
> Loads sessionInfo once and extracts the selected trials. No re-selection or
> re-ranking occurs. Keep curated to try different session counts later.
> All original neuron rows remain; selectedNeuronIDs is NOT applied automatically.
> For example:
> X = neuralCurated.byCondition.T1_S1_L.neuralRaw;
> T = neuralCurated.byCondition.T1_S1_L.trialInfo;
> % X(:,:,i) corresponds to row T(i,:).
> Xcommon = X(curated.tracking.selectedNeuronIDs,:,:);
>
> trialInfo sourceTrialRow indexes the input trialInfo table.
> metadataSessionRow (and tracking.sessions.sessionInfoRow) indexes sessionInfoBeh.
> sessionInfoRow in extracted trialInfoNeural indexes the neural sessionInfo table.
> These row indices may differ: sessionRec is used again during neural extraction.
> Empty neural arrays and invalid neural trial indices are reported separately in
> neuralCurated.droppedTrialReport; pre-extraction exclusions stay in
> neuralCurated.sessionExcludedTrials. Neural counts and trackedNeuronQC reflect
> sessions actually extracted, which can be fewer than the metadata prediction.
>
> Neural outputs retain neuralSelection, byCondition, qc, sessionAudit,
> droppedTrialReport, trackedNeuronQC, and optional neural QC figures. Metadata
> results leave those neural outputs empty and provide selectionQC instead.
> Custom cfg.normalizationFcn behavior is unchanged. RSA is not computed.
>
> For a single-call load, set cfg.loadNeural=true and cfg.sessionInfoFile to the
> large parsedTrial.mat. The old main_prepareRSAData(trialInfo,sessionInfo,cfg)
> in-memory call also works with cfg.loadNeural=true. To reproduce the old
> unranked selection, additionally set cfg.selectSessionsByTracking=false.
>
> Tests: analysis/trialSelection/tests/testModularRSACuration.m
> '@ | Set-Content -LiteralPath analysis/trialSelection/Modular_RSA_DataCuration_v2/README_v2.txt`

Implemented in `Modular_RSA_DataCuration_v2`. Start with [example_metadataTracking.m](./analysis/trialSelection/Modular_RSA_DataCuration_v2/example_metadataTracking.m).

```matlab
base = "B:\analysis\animal\zz159_PPC_parsedTrial";

cfg = defaultRSADataConfig();
cfg.trackingMethod = 'greedy'; % or 'error'
cfg.minSessionsPerStage = 3;
cfg.matchRemaining = true;

% Loads only trialInfo and sessionInfoBeh.
curated = main_prepareRSAData(base+"_trialInfo.mat", ...
    base+"_sessionInfoBeh.mat",cfg);

% Inspect the tracking curve, then choose a session count.
curated = chooseRSASessions(curated,12);

% Load the large file only when ready for neural activity.
neuralCurated = loadCuratedNeuralData(curated,base+".mat");
```

Key outputs:

- `curated.selection(k).trials`: selected trials per condition.
- `curated.tracking.selectedSessions`: chosen sessions, including `sessionRec`.
- `curated.tracking.selectedNeuronIDs`: neurons present across every chosen session.
- `curated.sessionExcludedTrials`: excluded trials and reasons.

Session selection supports phase-balanced greedy overlap or median-map squared error, without trial-count weighting. Neuron rows remain unchanged during extraction.

All **24 MATLAB tests passed**. On zz159 with the default filters, selecting 12 sessions retained **226 trials and 952 common neurons**, without loading the large parsed-trial file.

Implemented in [Animal.m](./organizer/object_organzier/Animal.m).

```matlab
ani = Animal('zz159_PPC');

cfg = fn_defaultTrialSelectionConfig();
curated = ani.fn_prepareTrialData(cfg);
curated = fn_chooseTrackedSessions(curated,12);
neural = ani.fn_loadCuratedNeuralData(curated);
```

The object retains `sessionInfoBeh` and `trialData`. `trialInfo` remains an alias for compatibility. **`sessionInfo` is no longer an object property.**

Storage is configured in `_param.m`:

- `myOps.sessionInfoName`: separate neural file.
- `myOps.animalObjectFile`: saved metadata object, variable `ani`.

Neural results are returned separately. Derived `dayInfo`, `chunkedDayInfo`, and `analysis` are omitted when saving the object.

Added [an object-based example](./analysis/trialSelection/trialCuration/example/example_animalTrialSelection.m). **All 32 tests passed**, including construction, persistence, metadata-only queries, and neural extraction.

> in animal.m, when creating a animal object without saved metadata, try to detect if there are any dayLabel in the _param.m; if there is; use that dayLabel as the dayLabel in sessionInfo matrix, instead of using the function to detect it.

Combined them into [fn_defaultTrialSelectionConfig.m](./organizer/trialCuration/config/fn_defaultTrialSelectionConfig.m). It now contains the learning periods, trial types, RT range, and tracking settings. Removed the separate builder.

For example:

```matlab
cfg = fn_defaultTrialSelectionConfig();

% Select S1/action1 and S2/action2.
cfg.conditionDef = cfg.conditionDef(1:2);
for k = 1:2
    cfg.conditionDef(k).dayLabels = ["T1E"; "T1M"];
end

cfg.RTRange = [0.35 0.65];
cfg.trackingMethod = 'greedy'; % or 'error'
cfg.minSessionsPerStage = 3;

curated = a.fn_prepareTrialData(cfg);
```

> what is the difference between first and second column
>
> User context
>
> Images:
> - `~/AppData/Local/Temp/codex-clipboard-2d66b560-a4fd-4f33-bca2-a0fd745f7554.png`

The first column is **`name`**: a code identifier used to access the output, for example:
```matlab
curated.byCondition.T1_S1_L
```
Keep it unique and use a valid MATLAB field name, without spaces or commas.

The second column is **`displayName`**: a readable label for reports and plots, such as `"T1, S1, L"`. It can contain spaces and punctuation.

Neither affects trial selection. The remaining columns—`dayLabels`, `taskLabels`, `stimulus`, and `action`—define which trials are selected.

> try a slightly different tracking session selection algorithm than greedy:
> for each next session selected, maximize (neurons shared with all previously selected sessions + 0.75*neurons shared (on average) with the next 5 best-matching in the remaining sessions). compare across all sessions posisble for the best session

>
> **Should the new method compare all remaining sessions from the start, or keep the current learning-stage balancing constraints?**
>
> Keep minimum sessions per stage, then compare all remaining sessions
>
> I’ll keep the minimum sessions per stage, then compare all remaining sessions. The lookahead term will use each candidate’s pairwise overlap with its five best remaining matches, excluding itself. With fewer than five left, it will average those available; with none left, that term will be zero.
>
> so, this new method is still subjective to the minimum session pre stage matching limit
>
> Yes. The minimum-per-stage requirement still applies. Within each stage’s turn, the new score chooses the best available session. Once every stage’s minimum is met, it compares all remaining sessions without stage balancing.
>

> </details>
>
> but let's say if we are picking a session past the minimum stage requirement, any sessions can be selected for comparison
>
> Exactly. After all stage minimums are met, every remaining eligible session is a candidate, regardless of learning stage. Each candidate is scored using its own five best matches from the other remaining sessions.

Exactly—and implemented as `lookahead`:

- **Before minimums are met:** choose within the required stage.
- **After minimums are met:** score every remaining session, regardless of stage.

```matlab
cfg.trackingMethod = 'lookahead';
cfg.minSessionsPerStage = 3;
cfg.lookaheadWeight = 0.75;
cfg.lookaheadSessions = 5;

curated = a.fn_prepareTrialData(cfg);
```

Each candidate gets:
```text
shared with all selected sessions
+ 0.75 × average pairwise overlap with its five best remaining matches
```

Scores and neighbor identities are saved in `curated.tracking.sessions`. **33 tests passed.**

> add two methods of selecting the number of sessions in tracking

> the second approach is to record a strcuture that records an experimenter gienve session number for each give animal, and this sturcture can be recoded in trial selection cfg file. this is tha manual appraoch

Implemented both analyses. Start with [example_expertTaskActionGeometry.m](./analysis/taskActionGeometry/example_expertTaskActionGeometry.m).

```matlab
% curated is your expert trial-selection output
if ~curated.neuralLoaded
    curated = a.fn_loadCuratedNeuralData(curated);
end

opts = fn_taskActionGeometryConfig();
opts.dayLabels = "Int";        % [] to include all selected periods
opts.timeWindow = [-0.5 1];    % [] for the whole trial
opts.frameRate = 15;           % Verify your recording rate
opts.nComponents = 5;

linear = fn_analyzeTaskActionSubspaces(curated,opts);
geometry = fn_analyzeTaskActionManifold(curated,opts);
```

- **Linear analysis:** ranks task, action, and interaction axes using contrast SVD. Returns variance captured per axis, subspace overlap, and predictive performance on held-out sessions. Axes are in `linear.task.axes` and `linear.action.axes`.
- **Trajectory geometry:** treats each time point as a point in neuron space. Returns crossvalidated distances, task/action angles, action parallelism across tasks, and interaction strength. PCA displays the trajectories; quantitative distances use all neurons. The distance approach follows [crossvalidated dissimilarity methods](https://www.diedrichsenlab.org/pubs/Walther_Neuroimage_2016.pdf).

Two interpretation details:
- Multiple axes describe coding changes over time. Setting `opts.averageTime=true` gives at most one axis per binary factor.
- Expert correct trials couple stimulus and action, so the action axis remains stimulus-confounded. Task defaults to **S1/S2 versus S3/S4**; use `opts.taskDefinition='context'` for recorded task labels.

Equations and output fields are documented in [README.txt](./analysis/taskActionGeometry/README.txt). All **12 synthetic tests passed**; figures were inspected. Real animal data have not yet been analyzed.

> **For the task axis, should “T1 versus T2” mean the stimulus set (S1/S2 versus S3/S4), or the recorded task context? These differ for T1-stimulus probes during T2 training.**
>
> Stimulus set: S1/S2 versus S3/S4

That matches the implemented default: `opts.taskDefinition = 'stimulusSet'`.

- **T1:** S1/S2, including probes during T2 training.
- **T2:** S3/S4.

No code changes are needed.

> how to look at geometry

Run the geometry analysis on your loaded curated data:

```matlab
opts = fn_taskActionGeometryConfig();
opts.taskDefinition = 'stimulusSet';
opts.dayLabels = "Int";       % Compare within interleaved sessions
opts.timeWindow = [-0.5 1];   % Seconds relative to alignment
opts.frameRate = 15;          % Verify recording rate
opts.nComponents = 2;

geometry = fn_analyzeTaskActionManifold(curated,opts);
```

The figure contains six panels:

| Panel | What to look for |
|---|---|
| **Mean trajectories** | Four task × action trajectories. Similar left-to-right displacement in both tasks suggests shared action coding. PCA is only a visualization. |
| **Crossvalidated distances** | Larger values indicate stronger separation between condition/time points. Near zero indicates little reliable separation; negative estimates are allowed. |
| **Reliable factor separation** | Positive task/action curves indicate reproducible coding across held-out sessions. Strong interaction suggests action coding depends on task. |
| **Contrast angles** | **Task vs action near 90°:** distinct directions. **Action across tasks near 0°:** the same action direction in both tasks. Near 180° indicates reversal. |
| **Shared action coding** | Positive values support an action direction shared across tasks. |
| **Task/action subspaces** | Principal angles near 90° indicate separated subspaces; near 0° indicate overlap. These depend on how many axes you retain. |

**For factorized coding**, look for the combination of reliable task and action signals, parallel action directions across tasks, low interaction, and separated task/action subspaces. Orthogonality alone is insufficient.

Inspect the numerical outputs with:

```matlab
geometry.timeGeometry
geometry.taskActionOverlap
geometry.interactionFraction

% Four-condition distance matrix, averaged over the selected time window
D = mean(geometry.cv.distanceByTime,3);
labels = geometry.data.cellNames;
figure;
heatmap(labels,labels,D);
title('Average crossvalidated squared distance');
```

Start with the interleaved period and compare several time windows. Remember that in the expert correct-trial selection, stimulus identity and action remain coupled, so “action coding” here can also reflect stimulus differences.

>
> Searched the web for `"nature12742" "supp" pdf ...`

**Yes: each axis in the code has one fixed weight per neuron, applied unchanged at every time point.** But I should distinguish the current implementation from the targeted dimensionality reduction in [Mante et al. (2013)](https://www.nature.com/articles/nature12742).

The current code finds **ranked choice axes and ranked task axes independently**. It does **not** alternate “choice first, then task orthogonal to choice, then another choice axis.”

Here is the math.

1. **An axis is a weighted combination of neurons.**

   At time \(t\), population activity is a vector \(x(t)\in\mathbb R^N\). An axis \(w\in\mathbb R^N\) produces:

   \[
   z(t)=w^\top x(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_i x_i(t).
   \]

   There are \(N\) neuron weights. **There are no learned time weights in this projection.** The same \(w\) produces a trajectory \(z(t)\) across the trial.

   By default, \(x\) contains normalized activity, so these weights refer to normalized neurons.

2. **The code constructs choice and task differences at every time.**

   Let \(\mu_{1L}(t),\mu_{1R}(t),\mu_{2L}(t),\mu_{2R}(t)\) be the four trial-averaged population responses.

   The choice contrast averages right-minus-left across tasks:

   \[
   d_C(t)=
   \frac{\mu_{1R}(t)+\mu_{2R}(t)
   -\mu_{1L}(t)-\mu_{2L}(t)}{2}.
   \]

   The task contrast averages T2-minus-T1 across choices:

   \[
   d_T(t)=
   \frac{\mu_{2L}(t)+\mu_{2R}(t)
   -\mu_{1L}(t)-\mu_{1R}(t)}{2}.
   \]

   Here, your task definition is **S1/S2 versus S3/S4**.

3. **The first choice axis maximizes squared choice separation across the selected times.**

   Specifically:

   \[
   w_{C,1}
   =
   \underset{\|w\|=1}{\arg\max}
   \sum_{t\in\mathcal W}\left[w^\top d_C(t)\right]^2.
   \]

   Each included time bin contributes equally. Squaring means that a difference can reverse sign over time without cancelling.

   Stack the contrasts into a neuron × time matrix:

   \[
   D_C=[d_C(t_1),\ldots,d_C(t_T)].
   \]

   Then the objective is \(w^\top D_CD_C^\top w\). Its maximizer is the first left singular vector of:

   \[
   D_C=U_C\Sigma_CV_C^\top.
   \]

   **The neuron weights are columns of \(U_C\).** Although SVD also returns temporal patterns in \(V_C\), the projection uses only \(U_C^\top x(t)\); it does not combine different times.

4. **The second choice axis captures the most remaining choice separation.**

   It solves the same optimization, with an additional constraint:

   \[
   w_{C,2}
   =
   \underset{\|w\|=1,\;w^\top w_{C,1}=0}{\arg\max}
   \sum_t[w^\top d_C(t)]^2.
   \]

   Subsequent axes must be orthogonal to all earlier **choice** axes.

   The task axes are computed separately using \(D_T\). Therefore:

   \[
   w_{T,1}^{\top}w_{C,1}
   \quad\text{can be nonzero.}
   \]

   This lets us measure whether the two representations overlap. The optimization maximizes contrast energy; it does not require an axis to encode only choice or only task.

5. **Variance captured follows directly from the singular values.**

   For choice axis \(k\):

   \[
   \text{fraction captured}_k
   =
   \frac{\sigma_{C,k}^{2}}{\sum_j\sigma_{C,j}^{2}}.
   \]

   This is the fraction of **choice-contrast energy**, not total single-trial variance. Multiple axes describe changes in the population direction of choice coding over time. If you average time first, \(D_C\) becomes one vector and has at most one nonzero axis.

The Mante paper uses **regression-derived directions**, denoises them using a PCA subspace, and orthogonalizes them to construct task-related coordinates. Its displayed axes are fixed neuron-weight vectors too. The paper’s procedure does not generate a sequence of first, second, third choice axes using the contrast SVD implemented here. [Paper, Extended Data Fig. 4](https://www.nature.com/articles/nature12742).

For a **choice-first, task-second orthogonal coordinate system**, the two-vector construction would be:

\[
q_C=\frac{b_C}{\|b_C\|},
\qquad
q_T=
\frac{b_T-q_C(q_C^\top b_T)}
{\|b_T-q_C(q_C^\top b_T)\|},
\]

where \(b_C,b_T\) are estimated coding directions. This assigns the shared component to choice and retains only the perpendicular component for task. Reversing the order changes the result.

**That distinction matters for your question about factorization:** orthogonalizing the axes makes them perpendicular by construction. To assess whether the representations naturally overlap, we must examine the directions **before** that orthogonalization. The current independent fits preserve that information.

One additional detail: `geometry` applies noise normalization across neurons before fitting its subspaces. Setting `opts.noiseMetric='identity'` makes its subspace metric match the linear analysis.

The IDs of neurons tracked across **all selected sessions** are stored in:

```matlab
neuronIDs = allTrialsel{i}.tracking.selectedNeuronIDs;
```

The corresponding logical mask is:

```matlab
neuronMask = allTrialsel{i}.tracking.selectedNeuronMask;
```

**The activity arrays still contain all neurons.** Select the tracked rows yourself:

```matlab
% Activity for condition k
X = allTrialsel{i}.neuralSelection(k).neuralRaw;
Xtracked = X(neuronIDs,:,:);   % Tracked neurons × time × trials

% Corresponding trial table
trials = allTrialsel{i}.neuralSelection(k).trialInfoNeural;
```

Or access a condition by name:

```matlab
X = allTrialsel{i}.byCondition.T1_S1_L.neuralRaw;
Xtracked = X(neuronIDs,:,:);
```

Use each animal’s own `neuronIDs`; neuron identities are separate between animals.

> I did this to select neural activity tracked for all sessions
> tempAct = {};
> for i = 1:12
>     tempAct(i,:) = cellfun(@(x)(x.neuralSelection(i).neuralRaw...
>         (x.tracking.selectedNeuronMask,:,:)),allTrialsel,'UniformOutput',false);
>
> end
>
> but there is an error type on this animal that does not T1R label and thus activity is []; so giving this error
> The logical indices in position 1 contain a true value outside of the array bounds.
>
> what is the simplist way to solve this

Check for empty activity before indexing. A nested loop is the simplest:

```matlab
tempAct = cell(12, numel(allTrialsel));

for i = 1:12                      % Conditions
    for a = 1:numel(allTrialsel)   % Animals
        X = allTrialsel{a}.neuralSelection(i).neuralRaw;

        if ~isempty(X)
            mask = allTrialsel{a}.tracking.selectedNeuronMask;
            tempAct{i,a} = X(mask,:,:);
        end
    end
end
```

Missing conditions remain `[]` in `tempAct`. The error occurs because the neuron mask contains `true` entries, but an empty activity array has no neuron rows to index.

Added both options to the standalone function and Animal method:

- `'ShowPlots', false` — default: no display.
- `'PlotSavePath', ''` — default: no PNG saving.

```matlab
% Default: no plots
neural = ani.fn_prepareNeuralData(trialSel);

% Display and save PNGs
neural = ani.fn_prepareNeuralData(trialSel, ...
    'ShowPlots', true, ...
    'PlotSavePath', 'B:\analysis\plots\expert');

% Standalone: save PNGs without displaying figures
neural = fn_prepareNeuralData(trialSel, sessionInfoFile, ...
    'PlotSavePath', 'B:\analysis\plots\expert');
```

Saved filenames are recorded in `neural.plotFiles`. These options control neural QC plots; trial-selection plotting remains separate.
