> so I am not that interested in how time contribute to variance as compared to task or action; it is not an apple to apple comparison. does it make sense if we evaluate how task and action contribute to variance at each individual time point (but will require a trial-by-trial analysis, instead of trial average?)
> think about it; dont change code yet

<details><summary>3 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll separate two questions: how task and action explain differences between condition means at each time, and how much of the total single-trial variability they explain. Those need different denominators. I won’t change the code.
>
> <details><summary>Searched the web</summary>
>
> Searched the web for `site.elifesciences.org/articles/10989 demixed principal component analysis variance noise marginalization ANOVA`
>
> </details>
>
> Context compaction
>
> Source: automatic
> Status: completed
</details>

Yes—**evaluating task and action at each time point better addresses your question.** At a fixed time, the response shared across conditions becomes the baseline. You then ask how much the responses differ because of task, action, or their interaction, without comparing those effects against the overall time course.

**Whether you need individual trials depends on the denominator.**

- **Using condition averages:** How much of the difference between the four condition means is associated with task versus action?
- **Using individual trials:** How much of the total trial-to-trial neural variability is explained by task versus action?

The second interpretation sounds closer to what you want. Condition averages hide within-condition variability, so a seemingly strong task effect could actually be small relative to the variability across trials.

At each time point \(t\), I would fit this model separately for each neuron:

\[
x_r(t)=\mu(t)+\beta_T(t)T_r+\beta_A(t)A_r+
\beta_{TA}(t)T_rA_r+\epsilon_r(t)
\]

Here:

- \(T=-1,+1\): T1 versus T2 **stimulus set**, using your chosen definition.
- \(A=-1,+1\): left versus right action.
- \(\beta_T\): task effect averaged over actions.
- \(\beta_A\): action effect averaged over tasks.
- \(\beta_{TA}\): interaction—whether the action difference changes between tasks.
- \(\epsilon\): remaining trial variability, including unmodeled behavior and session differences.

These are **neuron weights at each time point; time is not another predictor or weighted dimension**.

With equal weighting of the four task × action conditions, the population-level decomposition is:

\[
V_{\mathrm{total}}(t)=
V_T(t)+V_A(t)+V_{TA}(t)+V_{\mathrm{within}}(t)
\]

For example, the task contribution is the sum of squared task coefficients across neurons:

\[
V_T(t)=\sum_n\beta_{T,n}(t)^2
\]

Then plot:

\[
\frac{V_T(t)}{V_{\mathrm{total}}(t)},\qquad
\frac{V_A(t)}{V_{\mathrm{total}}(t)},\qquad
\frac{V_{TA}(t)}{V_{\mathrm{total}}(t)}
\]

These directly compare task and action using the same denominator at every time point. In practice, noisy estimated coefficients inflate squared effects, so I would validate these effects using independent trial splits rather than interpret their raw squared values as unbiased signal.

For your data, I would make three choices:

1. **Weight the four conditions equally.** Unequal trial counts should not make one task or action dominate. This does not require discarding extra trials.
2. **Calculate within each animal first.** Trials from different animals are not simultaneous population observations. We can subsequently summarize across animals or pool their variance components.
3. **Keep normalization fixed across time and conditions.** Normalizing separately at each time point would remove part of the amplitude change you want to measure.

There is also a useful distinction from geometry: **variance contribution tells you how strong each effect is; geometry tells you whether their neuron-weight vectors point in similar directions.** At each time, we can calculate the angle between \(\boldsymbol{\beta}_T(t)\) and \(\boldsymbol{\beta}_A(t)\). Each binary factor supplies one mean-contrast direction at a single time; additional dimensions arise when analyzing how those directions change across a time window.

My recommendation is therefore **time-resolved single-trial variance decomposition, accompanied by task–action angles**. Use the fully interleaved trials as planned. One interpretation limit remains: with only rewarded S1-left/S2-right and S3-left/S4-right trials, the action effect still includes stimulus identity differences.

No code changes yet.

Updated [fn_parseROI_2ch.py](./pythonPackageRockfish/registration/step0_convertH5/fn_parseROI_2ch.py) for your format:

- **Green:** pages 1, 3, 5, …
- **Red:** pages 2, 4, 6, …

Each channel is then split into the two ROIs.

> the tiff should have 3 axes, the 3rd axes is the frames, and it alternate g,r,g,r; so g should be 0:2:end

Yes—using zero-based indexing, green is `0, 2, 4, …` and red is `1, 3, 5, …`.

For a NumPy array shaped **Y × X × frames**:

```python
green = stack[:, :, 0::2]
red   = stack[:, :, 1::2]
```

The current script reads TIFF pages directly and already uses that same indexing:

```python
green_pages = np.arange(0, n_pages, 2)
red_pages   = np.arange(1, n_pages, 2)
```

So its channel selection matches your description.

> write a simple matlabCode here (no save) to take in a input Path e.g. 'F:\zz180_PPC\imagingSession'
> find the folders that contain 20260302 like
> 'zz180_20260302_2AFC1_parsed'
> and load Fall.mat in the subfolder
> 'F:\zz180_PPC\imagingSession\zz180_20260302_2AFC1_parsed\suite2p\plane0'
> get refImg = ops.refImg;
> save refImg as 'refimg.mat' in the second input to this code, e.g. 'G:\rockfish\ziyi\zz180_AC_2chan_redo', which contains subfolders matching like
> G:\rockfish\ziyi\zz180_AC_2chan_redo\zz180_20260302_2AFC1_parsed

```matlab
inputPath  = 'F:\zz180_PPC\imagingSession';
outputPath = 'G:\rockfish\ziyi\zz180_AC_2chan_redo';
dateText   = '20260302';

folders = dir(fullfile(inputPath, ['*' dateText '*']));
folders = folders([folders.isdir]);

for i = 1:numel(folders)
    sessionName = folders(i).name;
    sourceFile = fullfile(inputPath, sessionName, ...
        'suite2p', 'plane0', 'Fall.mat');
    outputFolder = fullfile(outputPath, sessionName);

    if ~isfile(sourceFile) || ~isfolder(outputFolder)
        warning('Skipping %s: source file or matching output folder missing.', ...
            sessionName);
        continue
    end

    data = load(sourceFile, 'ops');
    refImg = data.ops.refImg;

    save(fullfile(outputFolder, 'refimg.mat'), 'refImg');
    fprintf('Saved refimg.mat: %s\n', sessionName);
end
```

> matlab scipt to rename all files like zz180_PPC_session1_transform.txt into zz180_PPC_session01_transform for a given directory
> do not save the file; just print the code

```matlab
folderPath = 'B:\your\directory';

files = dir(fullfile(folderPath, '*_session*_transform.txt'));

for i = 1:numel(files)
    oldName = files(i).name;
    parts = regexp(oldName, '^(.*_session)(\d+)(_transform\.txt)$', ...
        'tokens', 'once');
    if isempty(parts), continue; end

    newName = sprintf('%s%02d%s', parts{1}, str2double(parts{2}), parts{3});
    if strcmp(oldName, newName), continue; end

    oldPath = fullfile(folderPath, oldName);
    newPath = fullfile(folderPath, newName);

    if isfile(newPath)
        warning('Skipping %s: %s already exists.', oldName, newName);
        continue
    end

    movefile(oldPath, newPath);
    fprintf('%s -> %s\n', oldName, newName);
end
```

> matlab code to take in one input folder and an output folder
> for each subfolder in output folder, find the subfolder of same name in input folder
> under that inputfolder/subfolder, make a copy of crossSessionSuite2p.mat into the outputfolder/subfolder

```matlab
inputFolder  = 'B:\input';
outputFolder = 'B:\output';

folders = dir(outputFolder);
folders = folders([folders.isdir] & ...
    ~ismember({folders.name}, {'.', '..'}));

for i = 1:numel(folders)
    sessionName = folders(i).name;
    sourceFile = fullfile(inputFolder, sessionName, 'crossSessionSuite2p.mat');
    targetFile = fullfile(outputFolder, sessionName, 'crossSessionSuite2p.mat');

    if ~isfile(sourceFile)
        warning('Source missing: %s', sourceFile);
        continue
    end

    if isfile(targetFile)
        warning('Already exists, skipping: %s', targetFile);
        continue
    end

    [ok, message] = copyfile(sourceFile, targetFile);
    if ok
        fprintf('Copied: %s\n', sessionName);
    else
        warning('Failed for %s: %s', sessionName, message);
    end
end
```

</details>

In [suite2p_transformation_apply.py](<./pythonPackageRockfish/registration/step3_elastix+suite2p application/suite2p_transformation_apply.py:56>), that `ops.npy` supplies the **image dimensions and registration settings**:

| Field | Used for |
|---|---|
| `Lx`, `Ly` | Reshape the binary movie into frames and calculate the frame count. |
| `block_size` | Reconstruct the nonrigid block grid. |
| `maxregshiftNR` | Check whether the saved nonrigid shifts exceed the expected limit; prints a warning. |

The **actual shifts** come from each session’s `crossSessionSuite2p.mat`:

```python
crossSessionAlign = scipy.io.loadmat(
    os.path.join(session_dir, 'crossSessionSuite2p.mat')
)
```

It uses:

- `xoff`, `yoff`: rigid shifts.
- `xoff1`, `yoff1`: nonrigid shifts for each block.

Those saved shifts are applied to every frame of `data_elastix.bin`, producing `data_suite2p.bin`. **It does not use `ops["refImg"]` or estimate new shifts at this step.**

Therefore, when copying transformations to another dataset, the supplied `ops.npy` must describe the matching image dimensions and the block grid used to generate those `crossSessionSuite2p.mat` files.

No code changed.

> shouldn't it use crossSessionsuite2p?

Yes—it **already uses `crossSessionSuite2p.mat` for the actual transformation**.

The two files provide different information:

- **`crossSessionSuite2p.mat`**: how far to shift the image and each nonrigid block (`xoff`, `yoff`, `xoff1`, `yoff1`).
- **`ops.npy`**: image dimensions and how those blocks are arranged (`Lx`, `Ly`, `block_size`).

Your current `crossSessionSuite2p.mat` does not include the block-grid settings, so the code still needs `ops.npy`.

We could make it self-contained by also saving `Lx`, `Ly`, and `block_size` into `crossSessionSuite2p.mat`, then loading everything from that file.

> what input does extractTC take other than .bin file

Besides `data_suite2p.bin`, [extract_TC.py](./pythonPackageRockfish/registration/step4_extractTC/extract_TC.py) reads **two files**:

| File | Information used |
|---|---|
| `<session>/suite2p/plane0/ops.npy` | `Lx` and `Ly` to read the binary frames. `block_size` is only printed. |
| `<base>/stackROI_final_tracked.mat` | `roiFinal`: the ROI polygon coordinates for each session and neuron. |

Expected structure:

```text
animal/
├── stackROI_final_tracked.mat
└── imagingSession/
    └── session_name/
        └── suite2p/plane0/
            ├── data_suite2p.bin
            └── ops.npy
```

**Session matching is positional:** it sorts the session folder names and uses the corresponding row of `roiFinal`. Those orders must match.

It averages the pixels inside each ROI for every frame and saves `data_F.mat`, containing `allTraces` as **frames × neurons**.

It does not load `crossSessionSuite2p.mat`, `refImg.mat`, or the cross-session registration `ops.npy`; the movie is expected to be already aligned.

> write a quick matlab script; right now I have 203 sessions, with these variables about stackROI
> I want to select a subset of 203 sessions (e.g. 102 to 109 sessions)
> write a function to take this subset for every variables with a size of 203 in at least one dimension
>
> User context
>
> Images:
> - `~/AppData/Local/Temp/codex-clipboard-1ec8657e-710b-4712-b936-06185b8cd960.png`

This subsets every dimension whose size is `203`, while leaving other variables unchanged. It works for numeric arrays and cell arrays such as `roiFinal`.

```matlab
% Load all variables into a structure, then keep sessions 102–109.
S = load('stackROI_final_tracked.mat');
Ssubset = fn_subsetSessions(S, 102:109, 203);

% Optional: save the subset as separate variables in a new MAT file.
% save('stackROI_subset.mat', '-struct', 'Ssubset');

function Sout = fn_subsetSessions(S, sessionIdx, nSessions)
    if nargin < 3
        nSessions = 203;
    end

    validateattributes(sessionIdx, {'numeric'}, ...
        {'vector','integer','positive','finite','<=',nSessions});

    Sout = S;
    names = fieldnames(S);

    for k = 1:numel(names)
        name = names{k};
        value = S.(name);
        sessionDims = find(size(value) == nSessions);

        if isempty(sessionDims)
            continue
        end

        indices = repmat({':'}, 1, ndims(value));
        for d = sessionDims
            indices{d} = sessionIdx;
        end

        Sout.(name) = value(indices{:});
        fprintf('%s: %s -> %s\n', name, ...
            mat2str(size(value)), mat2str(size(Sout.(name))));
    end
end
```

For your example, `1×203` becomes `1×8`, `203×911` becomes `8×911`, and `Pix` and `stepSize` remain unchanged.

> but when I want to save S, I need the individual field of S out so I can save in the same format

Use `'-struct'` to save each field as an individual variable, matching the original MAT-file format:

```matlab
save('stackROI_final_tracked_subset.mat', '-struct', 'Ssubset', '-v7.3');
```

If your structure is named `S`, use:

```matlab
save('stackROI_final_tracked_subset.mat', '-struct', 'S', '-v7.3');
```

No need to unpack the fields into the workspace first.

> in spkNorm, which direction does the normalization go on

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check how `spkNorm` is calculated and which matrix dimension it normalizes.
>
> <details><summary>Ran 2 commands</summary>
>
> - Ran `rg -n 'spkNorm|normSpk' organizer helper_function preprocess`
> - Ran `Get-Content 'organizer/object_organzier/Animal.m' | Select-Object -Skip 1348 -First 40; Get-Content 'organizer/object_organzier/Animal.m' | Select-Object -Skip 35 -First 48; Get-Content 'organizer/object_organzier/fn_readAnimalImaging.m' | Select-Object -First 130`
>
> </details>
</details>

In [Animal.m](/C:/Users/zzhu34/Documents/GitHub/analyzeMeso/organizer/object_organzier/Animal.m:1349), `spkNorm` normalizes **each neuron independently across time (dimension 1)**.

Each `actCell{i}` is **frames × neurons**. It:

1. Computes each neuron’s L2 norm within each recording:
   ```matlab
   norm(actCell{i}(:,j))  % sqrt(sum(activity.^2)), excluding nonfinite values
   ```
2. Averages these norms across recordings, weighted by their frame counts.
3. Divides every time point of that neuron, in every recording, by that same averaged norm.

For recording \(i\), neuron \(j\):
\[
a_j=\sum_i\frac{T_i}{\sum_kT_k}
       \sqrt{\sum_t x_{i,t,j}^{\,2}},
\qquad
x^\mathrm{normalized}_{i,t,j}=\frac{x_{i,t,j}}{a_j}.
\]

So it **does not normalize across neurons**, and it does not subtract a mean. It uses L2 magnitude, not RMS or z-score; consequently, long recordings can produce small normalized values.

> when it reports nan stats in reportActCellNan, when I run a script, it says (spk after normalization: 0.000% NaN values across 166 cells; 0 all-NaN cells.)
> but this animal has 166 sessions with >600 neurons.
> is there a dimension match up wrong somewhere?

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check whether that report counts session entries in the cell array or individual neurons, and verify the dimensions it uses.
>
> <details><summary>Ran a command</summary>
>
> - Ran `Get-Content 'organizer/object_organzier/Animal.m' | Select-Object -Skip 1348 -First 110; rg -n 'function .*loadTC|actCell\{' organizer/object_organzier/Animal.m`
>
> </details>
</details>

**That message does not indicate a dimension mismatch.** Here, “cells” means **MATLAB cell-array entries (sessions)**, not neurons. The wording is confusing.

The code uses:
```matlab
nCell = numel(actCell);  % 166 sessions
```

Each `actCell{i}` contains a **frames × neurons** matrix, so your >600 neurons are inside each of those 166 entries.

The report means:

- **166 cells:** 166 session matrices.
- **0.000% NaN:** the percentage across all frames and neurons in all sessions, rounded to three decimal places.
- **0 all-NaN cells:** no entire session matrix contains only NaNs. This does **not** mean there are zero all-NaN neuron traces.

To verify dimensions while paused inside `spkNorm`:
```matlab
size(actCell)       % Expected: 166 entries
size(actCell{1})    % Expected: [nFrames, nNeurons]

nNeurons = cellfun(@(x) size(x,2), actCell);
unique(nNeurons)    % Expected: one common neuron count, >600
```

The normalization treats columns as neurons; the reporting function counts all matrix elements regardless of orientation.

The figure handles weren’t the main issue. I confirmed that **the same neural activity is stored in four places** when normalization is disabled: raw and normalized arrays in both `neuralSelection` and `byCondition`. Your example also still uses compressed `-v7.3` saving.

Try saving a compact copy. This keeps all neurons, trial tables, tracking information, and configuration; your original variable stays unchanged.

```matlab
S.allTrialsel = allTrialsel;

for a = 1:numel(S.allTrialsel)
    x = S.allTrialsel{a};

    % Remove the redundant condition-organized copy.
    x = rmfield(x, intersect(fieldnames(x), {'byCondition','figures'}));

    if isfield(x.tracking,'figure')
        x.tracking = rmfield(x.tracking,'figure');
    end

    % Remove normalized arrays only when they duplicate the raw arrays.
    if isfield(x.neuralSelection,'neuralNorm')
        for k = 1:numel(x.neuralSelection)
            if strcmp(x.neuralSelection(k).normalizationApplied,'none')
                x.neuralSelection(k).neuralNorm = [];
            end
        end
    end

    S.allTrialsel{a} = x;
end

tic
save('B:\analysis\trialSelection\interleavedAnalysis\allTrialsel_compact.mat', ...
     '-struct','S','-v7.3','-nocompression');
toc

clear S x
```

**It can, but this screenshot alone doesn’t show that it’s causing the slow saves.** B: has **1.96 TB free out of 25.4 TB—about 7.7% free**.

Low free space can reduce write speed, depending on the storage:

- **SSD:** less free space can make sustained writes slower.
- **Hard drive:** fragmentation and where data is written can affect speed.
- **Network drive:** network bandwidth and server workload may matter more.

For your MATLAB save, duplicated neural arrays and compression are also potential bottlenecks. **Having 1.96 TB available doesn’t inherently make saving slow**, provided the file fits.

A useful comparison is to save the **same compact variable with `-nocompression`** to B: and a local SSD. If the local save is much faster, that points to the B: storage path as a bottleneck.

> for the neural subspace right now; how is it selecting the axes

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check the current subspace code and explain how it selects and orders the axes.

> <details><summary>Ran 2 commands</summary>

> - Ran `Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeTaskActionSubspaces.m; Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_taskActionGeometryConfig.m; rg -n 'svd|pca|task|action|orth|axisMethod' analysis/taskActionGeometry -g '*.m'`
> - Ran `Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_taskActionBasis.m; Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_taskActionMeans.m; Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_taskActionPopulationMeans.m; Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_taskActionMarginals.m; rg -n 'opts\.|intNames' analysis/step1_prepareNeuralData/example_prepareNeuralData.m`
>
> </details>
</details>

Currently, it finds **separate sets of task and action axes using SVD of trial-averaged contrasts**. Each axis has one weight per neuron, fixed across time.

Your current example uses **Int trials**, the **−0.5 to +0.2 s window**, z-score normalization, and up to **3 axes per factor**.

1. **Normalize and average trials.** Each neuron is z-scored using all selected time points and trials together. Then it calculates four condition means:
   \[
   C_{T1,L}(t),\quad C_{T1,R}(t),\quad C_{T2,L}(t),\quad C_{T2,R}(t).
   \]
   Each is a population activity vector at time \(t\). For multiple animals, these means are concatenated along the neuron dimension.

2. **Calculate task and action differences at each time point.**
   \[
   D_{\mathrm{task}}(t)=
   \frac{C_{T2,L}(t)+C_{T2,R}(t)-C_{T1,L}(t)-C_{T1,R}(t)}{2}
   \]
   \[
   D_{\mathrm{action}}(t)=
   \frac{C_{T1,R}(t)+C_{T2,R}(t)-C_{T1,L}(t)-C_{T2,L}(t)}{2}.
   \]
   These form two **neurons × time** matrices.

3. **Find the best neuron-weight axes for each difference matrix.** For task, the first axis solves:
   \[
   u_1=\arg\max_{\|u\|=1}
   \sum_t\left[u^\top D_{\mathrm{task}}(t)\right]^2.
   \]
   Thus, it maximizes the **summed squared task separation across the selected time window**. The second axis maximizes the remaining separation while being perpendicular to the first; subsequent axes follow the same rule. Action axes are fitted independently using the same procedure.

The implementation is in [fn_taskActionBasis.m](/C:/Users/zzhu34/Documents/GitHub/analyzeMeso/analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_taskActionBasis.m:3):
```matlab
[U,S,~] = svd(M,'econ');
axes = U(:,1:r);   % neurons × axes
```

**Important implications:**

- It does **not** alternate “best task axis, then best action axis.” Each factor gets its own independently ranked basis.
- Task axes are orthogonal to other task axes; **task and action axes can overlap**.
- Time points contribute to selecting the axes, but the resulting projection uses **only neuron weights**: `u' * activity(:,t)`.
- Axes maximize contrast energy, not classification accuracy or signal-to-noise ratio.
- Cross-validation assesses generalization, but the returned axes are fitted using all selected trials. `nComponents=3` requests up to three axes; it does not automatically choose a reliable dimensionality.

If you set `opts.averageTime=true`, the window is averaged first, leaving **at most one nonzero task axis and one action axis**.

> the same axis is selected in the manifold analysis?

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check whether the manifold analysis uses the same contrasts and preprocessing before selecting its axes.
>
> <details><summary>Ran a command</summary>
>
> - Ran `Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeTaskActionManifold.m | Select-Object -First 46; Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_taskActionNoiseMetric.m`
>
> </details>
</details>

**Same axis-selection method, but generally different axes with the default settings.**

Both use SVD of the task and action contrast matrices. However, the manifold analysis first applies **noise normalization**:

\[
D_{\text{linear}}=D,\qquad D_{\text{manifold}}=WD.
\]

By default, `opts.noiseMetric='diagonal'`: \(W\) divides each neuron’s activity by its estimated residual noise standard deviation. Therefore, noisier neurons contribute less to manifold axis selection.

To get matching task/action axes between the two analyses, use:
```matlab
opts.noiseMetric = 'identity';
opts.averageTime = false;

linear   = fn_analyzeTaskActionSubspaces(intAct, opts);
geometry = fn_analyzeTaskActionManifold(intAct, opts);
```

With identical inputs and other settings, these correspond:
```matlab
linear.task.axes       % Matches geometry.taskBasis.axes
linear.action.axes     % Matches geometry.actionBasis.axes
```

**The manifold’s 3D trajectory plot uses a separate set of PCA axes**, stored in `geometry.embedding.axes`. Those maximize total variation across all four condition trajectories; they are not the task/action axes. Its quantitative distances use the full neuron space, not just those three display axes.

```matlab
conditions = table( ...
    ["Int_S1_L";"Int_S2_R";"Int_S3_L";"Int_S4_R"], ...
    ["L";"R";"L";"R"], ["T1";"T1";"T2";"T2"], ...
    'VariableNames', {'conditionName','action','task'});

[cfg, plotCfg] = fn_neuralGeometryConfig();
cfg.method = 'lda';          % 'meanSquared' or 'regression'
cfg.nAxes = 3;               % Maximum per variable
cfg.normalization = 'none';

plotCfg.trialTypes = conditions.conditionName;
plotCfg.trajectory = 'both'; % 3D and three 2D views

[geometry, figures] = fn_analyzeNeuralGeometry( ...
    intAct, conditions, cfg, plotCfg);
```

**All retained neurons from all animals are pooled into one fit.** The main outputs are:

- `geometry.weights`: pooled neurons × axes.
- `geometry.neurons`: animal and original neuron-row mapping.
- `geometry.axisInfo`: variable and rank of each axis.
- `geometry.projection`: condition projections and trial variability.
- `geometry.variance`: variance summaries and time courses.
- `geometry.similarity`: axis angles and cosine similarity.

The sanity plots show **condition PSTHs with a shared peak-time neuron ordering**, plus marginalized population means using `fn_plotMeanErrorbar`. Shading is SEM across neuron-wise trial means.

> why is the axes soo correlated? when you take out the 1st axis; shouldn't you fit the remaining variance on a different axis?

> - `~/AppData/Local/Temp/codex-clipboard-72ed3668-9158-428b-abd2-80bd442b2216.png`

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check how the code removes each fitted axis and how the angle matrix is calculated, so I can explain what this plot shows.
>
> <details><summary>Ran 2 commands</summary>
>
> - Ran `rg -n 'deflat|orthog|invSw|similarity|angleDegrees|function.*fit|weights|residual' analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeNeuralGeometry.m`
> - Ran `$f=Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeNeuralGeometry.m; $f[48..110]; $f[144..154]; $f[265..315]`
>
> </details>
</details>

Yes—**after removing the first axis, the next axis is fitted in the remaining orthogonal neural space.** The code does this **separately for action and task**.

Your figure shows **angles, not correlations**:

- **Yellow ≈ 90°:** axes are orthogonal.
- **Purple ≈ 0°:** axes point along the same direction.
- The purple diagonal compares each axis with itself.

The action–action and task–task blocks are yellow off the diagonal, so the removal is working.

Within each variable, after selecting axes \(U=[u_1,\ldots,u_k]\), the next fit is restricted to the remaining space:
\[
X_{\mathrm{remaining}}=(I-UU^\top)X.
\]
Consequently, \(u_{k+1}^{\top}u_j=0\) for all preceding axes **of that variable**.

However, **task axes are fitted independently of action axes**. Task 1 does not have action 1–3 removed first. Thus, cross-variable entries can have smaller angles—such as action 3 versus task 3 in your plot. That overlap is precisely what this comparison measures.

If you instead want one sequence—**action 1 → task 1 → action 2 → task 2**, removing *every* previously selected axis—then we need to change the fitting procedure. All axes would then be orthogonal by construction, so this angle matrix would no longer tell you whether task and action naturally share neural dimensions.

Also, orthogonal **neuron weights** do not guarantee uncorrelated **projected activity traces**; activity covariance can still make those traces correlated.

> what is difference between variance and contributio nin the cariance plot

<details><summary>1 previous message</summary>

> <details><summary>Ran a command</summary>
>
> - Ran `Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeNeuralGeometry.m | Select-Object -Skip 304 -First 60`
>
> </details>
</details>

In the current figure, **“variance” refers to effects of variables such as task/action; “contribution” refers to individual trial conditions such as T1L/T1R.** Both are divided by the same total trial-to-trial variance on that axis.

For each neural axis and time point:

| Plot | What it measures |
|---|---|
| **Variables: variance / total** | How much variance is uniquely explained by **task** or **action**, after accounting for the other variables. |
| **Conditions: contribution / total** | How much each **condition’s mean response** contributes to the overall separation between conditions. |

For condition \(c\), its contribution is:
\[
\text{Contribution}_c(t)
=\frac{[\mu_c(t)-\bar{\mu}(t)]^2}
{N_{\mathrm{conditions}}\,V_{\mathrm{total}}(t)}.
\]

Here, \(\mu_c\) is the trial-average projection for that condition, and \(\bar\mu\) is the equally weighted average across conditions. Total variance includes both **differences between condition means** and **trial variability within conditions**.

Consequently:

- A condition gets a large contribution when its mean is far from the overall mean.
- This does **not** tell you whether that difference comes from task, action, or their interaction.
- Adding all condition contributions gives the dashed **“All conditions”** curve.
- Task and action’s unique explained fractions need not sum to that curve—for example, interactions can account for additional separation.

The labels would be clearer as **“Unique variance explained by variable”** and **“Condition contribution to between-condition variance.”**

> what is difference between correlation and cosine? should they be similar (dot product) if they are unit vector
>
> User context
>
> Images:
> - `~/AppData/Local/Temp/codex-clipboard-3d5beed8-e7af-4ac6-bb92-ef41531527ae.png`

**Cosine is the dot product of unit vectors. Pearson correlation is the cosine after subtracting each vector’s mean.** Unit length alone does not make them equal.

For neuron-weight vectors \(u,v\):

\[
\text{cosine}(u,v)=\frac{u^\top v}{\|u\|\|v\|}
\]

\[
\text{correlation}(u,v)=
\frac{(u-\bar u\mathbf1)^\top(v-\bar v\mathbf1)}
{\|u-\bar u\mathbf1\|\;\|v-\bar v\mathbf1\|}
\]

Here, the mean is taken **across neurons**. Subtracting it changes the axis direction.

For example, these unit vectors are orthogonal:
\[
u=[1,0],\qquad v=[0,1].
\]
Their cosine is **0**. After centering, they become \([0.5,-0.5]\) and \([-0.5,0.5]\), so their correlation is **−1**.

They are equal when **both weight vectors have zero mean**, and typically close when those means are sufficiently small. Unit-length normalization does not impose zero mean.

**For your question about neural-axis overlap and orthogonality, cosine is the appropriate measure.** Pearson correlation instead compares the pattern of relative weights after removing each axis’s uniform population component.

```matlab
plotCfg.similarity = 'cosine';
```

With this setting, the orthogonal axes within each variable should have off-diagonal values near zero—white in the plot.

> but it seems that you do the axis with slightly different metric?
>

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check the current implementation against that formula, particularly the covariance estimate and how subsequent axes are fitted.
>
> <details><summary>Ran a command</summary>
>
> - Ran `$f=Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeNeuralGeometry.m; $f[52..97]; $f[272..300]; Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_neuralGeometryConfig.m | Select-Object -First 10`

> </details>
</details>

Your formula is correct:
\[
w=\Sigma^{-1}(\mu_1-\mu_0).
\]

**The first axis in our code uses the same LDA principle. The differences are how we estimate \(\Sigma\), regularization, and the additional axes.**

The objective I described,
\[
\max_w\frac{[w^\top(\mu_1-\mu_0)]^2}{w^\top\Sigma w},
\]
has the solution \(w\propto\Sigma^{-1}(\mu_1-\mu_0)\). These are equivalent formulations.

In the current default code:

\[
\boxed{
u_1=
\frac{[\operatorname{diag}(\Sigma_w)+\lambda I]^{-1}(\mu_1-\mu_0)}
{\left\|[\operatorname{diag}(\Sigma_w)+\lambda I]^{-1}(\mu_1-\mu_0)\right\|}
}
\]

Specifically:

- **Diagonal covariance:** the default ignores correlations between neurons. Each neuron’s weight is proportional to its mean difference divided by its regularized within-class variance:
  \[
  w_n\propto\frac{\mu_{1,n}-\mu_{0,n}}{\sigma_{w,n}^2+\lambda}.
  \]
- **Regularization:** \(\lambda I\) stabilizes the covariance estimate.
- **Unit-length normalization:** changes the weight magnitude, not the axis direction.
- **Pooling:** means and within-class variances include all selected time points and trials, with balanced condition weights.

To include neuron–neuron covariance within each animal:
```matlab
cfg.covariance = 'withinAnimal';
```
Cross-animal covariance remains zero because those neurons were not recorded on paired trials.

**The second and third axes are the nonstandard extension.** Conventional binary LDA gives one discriminant direction. Our code removes each previous direction and refits in the remaining orthogonal space, as you requested. Those are residual-separation axes, not additional conventional LDA discriminants.

So your concern is justified: the default is **regularized diagonal LDA**, rather than full-covariance LDA. It is not a different separation objective, but the covariance approximation can substantially change the resulting axis.

> what is the math on identifying residual-separation axes
> and does that make sense mathematically

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll derive the constrained fit and explain what the extra axes represent. They have a valid mathematical objective, but that does not necessarily mean they contain additional task or action information.
>
> <details><summary>Searched the web</summary>
>
> Searched the web for `https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/lda_qda.html`
>
> </details>
</details>

**Yes, it is mathematically valid as a constrained optimization. But the extra axes are not necessarily independent dimensions of task/action information.** That distinction matters for interpreting your results.

Let
\[
d=\mu_1-\mu_0
\]
be the population mean difference, and \(S\) be the covariance matrix used by the code, including its diagonal/block approximation and regularization.

The first axis solves
\[
u_1=\arg\max_{\|u\|=1}
\frac{(u^\top d)^2}{u^\top S u},
\]
giving
\[
u_1=\frac{S^{-1}d}{\|S^{-1}d\|}.
\]

**For the second axis, we impose an additional constraint:**
\[
u_2=\arg\max_{\|u\|=1,\;u^\top u_1=0}
\frac{(u^\top d)^2}{u^\top S u}.
\]

So it asks: *among directions perpendicular to the first axis, which has the strongest remaining mean separation relative to variance?*

After selecting \(U=[u_1,\ldots,u_k]\), the next axis must satisfy \(U^\top u=0\).

One way to derive the solution is to choose an orthonormal basis \(Q\) for the remaining space:
\[
Q^\top U=0,\qquad Q^\top Q=I.
\]
Write \(u=Qa\). The problem becomes ordinary LDA in those remaining coordinates:
\[
a\propto(Q^\top S Q)^{-1}Q^\top d,
\]
and therefore
\[
\boxed{
u_{k+1}\propto Q(Q^\top S Q)^{-1}Q^\top d.
}
\]

The code computes the equivalent expression without constructing \(Q\):
\[
\boxed{
u_{k+1}\propto
\left[
S^{-1}
-S^{-1}U(U^\top S^{-1}U)^{-1}U^\top S^{-1}
\right]d.
}
\]
It then normalizes the result to unit length.

**Why can separation remain after removing the first LDA axis?**

Because the LDA direction \(S^{-1}d\) generally does **not** point along the mean-difference vector \(d\). LDA downweights noisy directions.

For example:
\[
d=\begin{bmatrix}1\\1\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
S=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\0&9\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Then
\[
u_1=\frac{1}{\sqrt{82}}\begin{bmatrix}9\\1\end{bmatrix}.
\]

The orthogonal second direction is
\[
u_2=\frac{1}{\sqrt{82}}\begin{bmatrix}-1\\9\end{bmatrix},
\]
and it still separates the means:
\[
u_2^\top d=\frac{8}{\sqrt{82}}\ne0.
\]

However, the two projected responses have correlated within-class fluctuations:
\[
u_1^\top S u_2=\frac{72}{82}\ne0.
\]

Thus, **orthogonal weight vectors do not imply independent information.**

Under the standard two-class, shared-covariance Gaussian model, the first LDA score already contains everything needed for the optimal class decision. Additional orthogonal projections do not add class information conditional on that score. Conventional binary LDA consequently has one discriminant dimension. [LDA mathematical formulation](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/lda_qda.html#mathematical-formulation-of-lda-dimensionality-reduction)

An illuminating special case is \(S=\sigma^2I\): then \(u_1\) points exactly along \(d\), and removing it leaves **zero** mean separation. Extra residual axes can therefore arise from unequal noise levels, rather than multiple independent coding dimensions.

**For your scientific question, I would distinguish two goals:**

- **Find the best time-pooled task/action discriminator:** use the first LDA axis.
- **Find several neural dimensions expressing task/action differences throughout the trial:** retain the time-dependent mean contrasts,
  \[
  D_{\mathrm{time}}=[d(t_1),d(t_2),\ldots,d(t_T)],
  \]
  and fit a low-dimensional basis to that matrix, optionally accounting for noise.

The second approach allows multiple dimensions because the task/action contrast can change direction over time. The resulting axes still have **weights only on neurons**, fixed across time. It is a more direct way to study multidimensional temporal coding than repeatedly extracting residual directions from one time-averaged contrast.

I would treat the current residual axes as **descriptive complementary views**, rather than evidence for multiple independent task/action dimensions.

> so here is the thing: my neural ensemble, although assumed to be gaussianly distributed when using LDA, is no where near gaussian. does it make sense to keep identifying more axes in the remaining activity?

<details><summary>1 previous message</summary>

> Context compaction
>
> Source: automatic
> Status: completed
</details>

**Yes, additional axes can be useful—but non-Gaussian activity alone is not a reason to keep extracting them.** The key question is whether those axes reveal additional, reproducible structure.

LDA has two distinct interpretations:

- **A Gaussian classification model:** assumes a Gaussian distribution within each class and a shared covariance.
- **Fisher’s separation criterion:** chooses a direction that maximizes
  \[
  J(w)=\frac{[w^\top(\mu_1-\mu_0)]^2}{w^\top S_Ww}.
  \]
  This optimization only needs means and covariances; **the activity does not need to be Gaussian**. Without Gaussian assumptions, it simply may not be the best possible classifier.

For your current residual-axis procedure, each additional axis asks:

> After removing the previous neural directions, which remaining direction has the strongest mean difference relative to its variability?

That is mathematically valid, even for non-Gaussian activity. However, **a second orthogonal axis does not necessarily contain new task information**. Orthogonal neural weights can produce correlated projected activity, and subsequent axes can represent another view of the same mean difference. Conversely, differences in distribution shape or variance with identical means would be missed by this criterion.

For your experiment, I think the stronger reason to extract multiple axes is **changing population coding over time**. For example:

- Early task separation may involve one group of neurons.
- Later task separation may involve another group.
- Pooling all time points into one mean difference can obscure that change.

You can retain this structure by constructing

\[
D=
\begin{bmatrix}
\mu_{T1}(t_1)-\mu_{T2}(t_1)&
\cdots&
\mu_{T1}(t_T)-\mu_{T2}(t_T)
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Then identify several neural directions that capture this separation across time, optionally accounting for within-condition variability. **Each axis still has one fixed weight per neuron; the weights do not change with time.** Multiple dimensions now reflect distinct patterns of task separation across the trial.

My recommendation is to keep residual Fisher axes as an exploratory option, but use time-resolved contrast dimensions for your main multidimensional geometry analysis. In either case, fit axes on training trials and evaluate separation on held-out trials—ideally holding out sessions when feasible. Additional axes are convincing when they capture reproducible held-out structure, rather than merely separating the data used to fit them.

> what does this mean:
> a second orthogonal axis does not necessarily contain new task information
> and
> Orthogonal neural weights can produce correlated projected activity, and subsequent axes can represent another view of the same mean difference.

An axis is a set of weights on neurons. **Two axes being perpendicular describes the weights—not whether their projected activity carries independent information.**

Suppose you have two neurons:

\[
w_1=\begin{bmatrix}1\\0\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
w_2=\begin{bmatrix}0\\1\end{bmatrix}.
\]

These axes are orthogonal because \(w_1^\top w_2=0\). Their projections are simply

\[
z_1=x_1,\qquad z_2=x_2.
\]

But if these neurons have similar activity, \(x_2\approx x_1\), then **the two projections are highly correlated despite their orthogonal weights**.

More generally, if neural activity has covariance \(\Sigma\),

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(z_1,z_2)=w_1^\top\Sigma w_2.
\]

Orthogonality gives \(w_1^\top w_2=0\), which does **not** imply \(w_1^\top\Sigma w_2=0\).

**What does “no new task information” mean?**

Imagine both neurons respond approximately like this:

| Task | Neuron 1 | Neuron 2 |
|---|---:|---:|
| T1 | Low | Low |
| T2 | High | High |

Both axes separate the tasks. But if neuron 2 merely copies neuron 1—including its fluctuations—then knowing neuron 2 adds little once you already know neuron 1.

The relevant question is therefore:

> Does axis 2 help distinguish the tasks **after we already know the activity on axis 1**?

That is different from asking whether axis 2 distinguishes tasks by itself. If the neurons have independent noise, the second neuron *could* help; redundancy is a possibility, not a consequence of orthogonality.

**How this applies to removing the first axis**

Your residual operation is

\[
x_{\mathrm{res}}=(I-w_1w_1^\top)x,
\]

for a unit-length \(w_1\). It removes the component along that direction. It does **not** remove every component statistically related to its activity or to the task.

The residual task mean difference is

\[
d_{\mathrm{res}}=(I-w_1w_1^\top)(\mu_{T1}-\mu_{T2}).
\]

Because the first LDA axis is proportional to \(\Sigma_W^{-1}d\), it generally is **not parallel to \(d\)**. Consequently, \(d_{\mathrm{res}}\) can remain nonzero, allowing a second axis to separate tasks using the remaining component of that same mean difference.

So your residual procedure does find a **different neural direction with remaining separation**. What it does not establish by itself is that this direction supplies additional task information. To test that, compare held-out task prediction using axis 1 alone versus axes 1 and 2 together.

> yes; it just mean that there are two groups of neurons (corresponding to their respective axes) but demonstrating similar temporal response (but still coming from orthognal axes)

Yes—that is a valid interpretation. **Distinct, orthogonal neural population patterns can express similar temporal responses and task selectivity.** That can be biologically interesting even if their information is redundant.

One correction: each axis is a **weighted combination of neurons**, rather than necessarily a separate group. Orthogonal axes can involve the same neurons with different positive and negative weights. Separate, nonoverlapping groups are one special case.

For your goal, extracting successive residual-separation axes therefore makes sense: it asks whether task or action separation remains in other neural directions after removing the first. Just distinguish:

- **Different neural directions expressing task/action separation:** your procedure identifies this.
- **Additional predictive information beyond earlier axes:** requires a separate evaluation.

Similar temporal responses across those axes do not invalidate their orthogonality or make the additional axes uninteresting.

> </details>
</details>

**Yes—identifying several neural axes associated with the same task variable has strong precedent in neuroscience.** However, the specific procedure we implemented—repeatedly maximizing Fisher separation while constraining each new axis to be orthogonal to earlier axes—should be distinguished from those established neuroscience methods.

These are the most relevant references:

| Method / paper | What it identifies | Relationship to your analysis |
|---|---|---|
| **Model-based targeted dimensionality reduction (mTDR), Aoi, Mante & Pillow, 2020** | Multiple neural dimensions encoding each variable, including choice and context, across time | Closest to your scientific question: whether the same variable is expressed through several distinct population patterns |
| **Demixed PCA (dPCA), Kobak et al., 2016** | Multiple components associated with stimulus, decision, time, and their interactions | Similar goal, but optimizes reconstruction and demixing rather than Fisher separation |
| **Targeted dimensionality reduction, Mante et al., 2013** | Regression-derived axes for choice, motion, color, and context, subsequently orthogonalized | Related supervised axes, but not repeated residual LDA for the same binary variable |

**The Aoi paper is particularly relevant.** They reanalyzed the context-dependent decision-making dataset and found that individual task variables occupied multidimensional subspaces, involving two or more orthogonal population patterns over time. Thus, a single “choice axis” could miss important structure. Their method uses low-rank regression with temporal structure, rather than sequentially fitting LDA to residual activity. [Aoi et al., *Nature Neuroscience*, 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7610668/)

**dPCA also supports extracting several components per task variable.** It separates task-dependent response structure into low-dimensional representations. Its decoding axes need not be orthogonal, and its objective differs from your signal-to-noise separation criterion. [Kobak et al., *eLife*, 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4887222/)

**Mante 2013 is related, but does not directly justify “choice axis 1, choice axis 2, choice axis 3” from residual LDA.** Its original analysis orthogonalizes regression vectors corresponding to different task variables. [Mante et al., *Nature*, 2013](https://www.nature.com/articles/nature12742)

For **your exact mathematical approach**, a closer precedent is **orthogonal linear discriminant analysis (OLDA)** in the machine-learning literature. This family of methods successively optimizes discriminant directions subject to orthogonality constraints and can extract more than \(C-1\) directions. An example is [*Fast orthogonal linear discriminant analysis with application to image classification*, 2015](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0925231215000909). That is a methodological precedent, **not a neuroscience application**.

I did not find a clear neuroscience example using precisely our current residual-Fisher implementation. I would therefore describe your method as **“sequential orthogonal Fisher discriminant axes”**, explicitly state its objective, and evaluate held-out separation. The neuroscience papers support studying multiple dimensions per variable; they should not be cited as using this exact algorithm.

> right now is it taking covariance within each animal?

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check how the current code computes covariance, including whether the default uses correlations between neurons within each animal.
>
> <details><summary>Ran 2 commands</summary>
>
> - Ran `rg -n "covariance|withinAnimal|diagonal|ridge|Sw|inverse" analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeNeuralGeometry.m analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_neuralGeometryConfig.m`
> - Ran `$p='analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeNeuralGeometry.m'; $s=Get-Content $p; $s[53..95]; $s[230..250]; Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_neuralGeometryConfig.m | Select-Object -First 14; rg -n "covariance" analysis/taskActionGeometry/example_neuralGeometry.m`
>
> </details>
</details>

**By default, no—it uses each neuron’s variance, but ignores covariance between different neurons**, including neurons within the same animal.

The current default in [fn_neuralGeometryConfig.m](./analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_neuralGeometryConfig.m:6) is:

```matlab
cfg.covariance = 'diagonal';
```

You can enable covariance within each animal with:

```matlab
cfg.covariance = 'withinAnimal';
```

Then the pooled covariance is block diagonal:

\[
\Sigma_W=
\begin{bmatrix}
\Sigma_{W,1}&0&0\\
0&\Sigma_{W,2}&0\\
0&0&\Sigma_{W,3}
\end{bmatrix},
\]

where each block contains neuron–neuron covariance within one animal. Cross-animal covariance is assumed zero because trials are not paired across animals. **All animals’ neurons still contribute to the same fitted axes.**

One important detail: the code pools **time points and trials** before computing covariance, subtracting each condition’s pooled mean. Therefore, this covariance includes shared temporal dynamics as well as trial-to-trial fluctuations—it is not purely trial-to-trial noise covariance after subtracting the PSTH.

> is this a good metric to use for umap:
> cfg.nNeighbors=80; cfg.minDist=0.4; cfg.metric='correlation';

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check how these settings fit your trial-averaged embedding and what structure correlation distance emphasizes.
>
> <details><summary>Ran a command, searched the web</summary>
>
> Searched the web for `https://umap-learn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/parameters.html`
>
> - Ran `rg -n "nNeighbors|minDist|metric|populationVectors|pointInfo|nPoints" analysis/taskActionGeometryEmbedding/fn_analyzeNeuralEmbedding.m analysis/taskActionGeometryEmbedding/fn_neuralEmbeddingConfig.m`
>
> </details>
</details>

**Yes—these are reasonable starting settings for visualizing population response patterns across conditions and time.** Correlation distance is appropriate if you care more about *which neurons are relatively active* than overall response amplitude.

In your code, each point is one **condition × time bin**, represented by a vector across all pooled neurons. Correlation distance compares two such vectors:

\[
d(x,y)=1-\operatorname{corr}(x,y).
\]

The correlation is **across neurons**, not across time.

| Setting | Interpretation for your data |
|---|---|
| `nNeighbors=80` | Uses relatively broad neighborhoods. With 4 conditions × 100 time bins = 400 points, this is a substantial neighborhood; small local differences may become less prominent. |
| `minDist=0.4` | Encourages more spread-out embeddings than `0.1`, making trajectories easier to inspect and reducing tight clumping. |
| `metric='correlation'` | Emphasizes the relative population activity pattern, removing each point’s across-neuron mean and normalizing its centered magnitude when calculating distance. |

Larger neighborhoods emphasize broader structure, while larger `minDist` discourages tight packing. [UMAP parameter documentation](https://umap-learn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/parameters.html)

**The main scientific tradeoff is amplitude sensitivity.** If

\[
y=2x+5\mathbf{1},
\]

then correlation distance is zero: the population pattern is identical despite a substantial increase in activity. Therefore:

- For **task/action population-pattern geometry**, correlation is a sensible choice.
- For **reward-related increases in population activity**, correlation can discard the effect you want to study; compare with Euclidean distance.

I would keep your settings as the initial visualization, then check robustness with:

```matlab
% Compare these choices; keep other settings fixed.
cfg.nNeighbors = 30;           % Compare against 80
cfg.minDist = 0.4;
cfg.metric = 'correlation';    % Compare against 'euclidean'
```

Look for conclusions that persist across reasonable settings, rather than selecting the embedding with the clearest condition separation. Separation in the 2-D plot alone does not establish task/action factorization.

> what does `minDist=0.4`Encourages more spread-out embeddings than `0.1`, making trajectories easier to inspect and reducing tight clumping.  **mean mathematically**

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check the distance-to-similarity formula so I can explain exactly where `minDist` enters.
>
> <details><summary>Searched the web</summary>
>
> Searched the web for `https://umap-learn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/_modules/umap/umap_.html`
>
> </details>
</details>

**`minDist` controls how close points need to be in the 2-D embedding to count as strongly similar. It is not a hard minimum distance.**

Let \(y_i,y_j\) be two embedded points, with distance

\[
r=\|y_i-y_j\|_2.
\]

UMAP converts this distance into a similarity:

\[
q(r)=\frac{1}{1+a r^{2b}}.
\]

The parameters \(a,b\) are fitted to approximate this target curve:

\[
q_{\mathrm{target}}(r)=
\begin{cases}
1,&r\le m,\\[2mm]
\exp\!\left[-\dfrac{r-m}{s}\right],&r>m,
\end{cases}
\]

where \(m=\texttt{minDist}\) and \(s=\texttt{spread}\). The actual optimization uses the smooth \(q(r)\), rather than this piecewise curve. [UMAP implementation: `find_ab_params`](https://umap-learn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/_modules/umap/umap_.html#find_ab_params)

With `spread=1`, the **target similarities** are:

| Embedded distance \(r\) | `minDist=0.1` | `minDist=0.4` |
|---|---:|---:|
| 0.1 | 1 | 1 |
| 0.4 | \(e^{-0.3}\approx0.74\) | 1 |
| 0.8 | \(e^{-0.7}\approx0.50\) | \(e^{-0.4}\approx0.67\) |

Thus, with `minDist=0.4`, **two neighboring points can remain farther apart while still having high similarity in the embedding**.

UMAP balances preserving strong neighbor relationships against separating points that are not neighbors. When high similarity extends over a wider distance range, neighboring points have less need to crowd into a tiny region. This generally produces less compact clusters. [UMAP optimization explanation](https://umap-learn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/how_umap_works.html)

For your neural trajectories:

- `0.1`: neighboring condition–time points can form tighter clumps.
- `0.4`: those neighborhoods typically occupy more space.
- Neither value explicitly smooths trajectories or uses temporal order.
- **Points can still be closer than 0.4.** The value is an embedding-scale parameter, not a neural correlation threshold or a distance in seconds.

My earlier “easier to inspect” statement was a possible visualization benefit, not a mathematical guarantee.

> so these are the cosine values ( I have the setting as cosine). a cosine value of 0 corresponds to 90 degrees
>
> User context
>
> Images:
> - `~/AppData/Local/Temp/codex-clipboard-d0b0af53-f2e4-409e-aad8-5377b9f67efe.png`

Yes. For unit-length axis weight vectors,

\[
\text{cosine similarity}=w_i^\top w_j=\cos\theta.
\]

| Cosine | Angle | Meaning |
|---:|---:|---|
| \(1\) | \(0^\circ\) | Same direction |
| \(0.5\) | \(60^\circ\) | Partially aligned |
| \(0\) | \(90^\circ\) | Orthogonal |
| \(-0.5\) | \(120^\circ\) | Partially aligned with opposite orientation |
| \(-1\) | \(180^\circ\) | Opposite direction along the same axis |

In your figure, the white off-diagonal entries within **action 1–3** and within **task 1–3** are expected: the algorithm enforces orthogonality within each set.

The action–task entries are not constrained to zero, so their colors show the measured alignment between those axes. For example, action 3 and task 3 appear more aligned than action 1 and task 1.

Remember that an axis’s sign can be flipped without changing the line it represents. For comparing **axis orientation regardless of sign**, use:

\[
\theta_{\mathrm{unsigned}}=\cos^{-1}\!\left(\left|w_i^\top w_j\right|\right),
\]

which ranges from \(0^\circ\) for the same line to \(90^\circ\) for orthogonal lines.

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check the calculation to confirm what the percentage measures and how it combines time points.
>
> <details><summary>Ran 2 commands</summary>
>
> - Ran `rg -n -C 5 "separationCapture|separationTotal|separation" analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeNeuralGeometry.m analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_plotNeuralGeometry.m`
> - Ran `$s=Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeNeuralGeometry.m; $s[126..148]`
>
> </details>
</details>

**Yes—specifically, it is the fraction of the squared L/R mean-response separation captured by that axis.** It is not classification accuracy or the fraction of total neural activity variance.

At each time point, the code computes the population mean responses for L and R, averaging equally over the included conditions within each action:

\[
d(t)=\mu_L(t)-\mu_R(t).
\]

This vector contains one difference per neuron, pooled across animals. For unit-length axis \(w_k\),

\[
\boxed{
\text{Separation captured}_k(t)
=
\frac{\left[w_k^\top d(t)\right]^2}
{\|d(t)\|^2}
}
\]

The numerator is the **squared L/R difference after projection**. The denominator is the **squared L/R difference in the full neural space**.

The two plots summarize this differently:

- **Time-course plot:** shows the fraction above at each time point.
- **Bar plot:** uses
  \[
  \frac{\sum_t [w_k^\top d(t)]^2}
       {\sum_t \|d(t)\|^2}.
  \]
  Consequently, times with stronger L/R separation contribute more; it is not a simple average of the time-course fractions.

In your action plot, the bars appear approximately **17%, 5%, and 3%**. Because the action axes are mutually orthogonal, together they capture approximately **25% of the squared L/R separation** over the analyzed time window.

One important distinction: **LDA selects axes using separation relative to within-class variability, whereas this plot measures separation alone.** Therefore, the best LDA axis need not capture the largest possible fraction of raw squared separation.

**The upper plot does not mean axis 1 captures 40% of the whole population’s trial variance.** It means **action explains about 40% of the variance already projected onto axis 1**, at that time point. The label “Variance / total” is ambiguous.

The denominators differ:

| Plot | What it measures |
|---|---|
| **Upper: action variance fraction** | Action-related variance on axis 1 ÷ total variance **on axis 1** |
| **Lower: separation captured** | L/R separation energy on axis 1 ÷ L/R separation energy **across the entire neural population** |

The upper calculation accounts for task through a joint regression. Its denominator includes both differences between condition means and trial-to-trial variability on that axis.

For a balanced action × task design, imagine these values at one time point:

- Total L/R separation energy across the population: **100 units**
- L/R separation energy captured on axis 1: **15 units**
- Total projected variance on axis 1: **37.5 units**

Then both are true:

\[
\text{Action fraction within axis 1}
=\frac{15}{37.5}=40\%
\]

\[
\text{Population action separation captured}
=\frac{15}{100}=15\%.
\]

So **axis 1 can have relatively clean action selectivity while containing only a modest portion of all action-related population activity**. Other neural directions may contain substantial L/R separation but also more variability.

There is also a time-summary difference in your screenshot: the upper value is at a particular time, whereas the lower bar combines separation energy across the full analyzed time window.

> what is your percentage of variance calculation? seems really low

<details><summary>2 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll check the exact calculation and scaling so I can explain why the values are low.
>
> <details><summary>Ran a command</summary>
>
> - Ran `$s=Get-Content analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_analyzeNeuralGeometry.m; $s[109..141]; $s[310..355]; rg -n 'Population|population total|100' analysis/taskActionGeometry/fn_plotNeuralGeometry.m`
>
> </details>
</details>

**The values are fractions, not percentages: `0.01` means 1%.** They can be low because the denominator includes trial-to-trial variability from **every neuron**, while the numerator includes only the action/task signal captured on **one axis**.

At each time point, the calculation is:

\[
F_{k,\mathrm{action}}(t)=
\frac{\text{Unique action-related variance on axis }k}
{\text{Total population variance}}.
\]

The denominator is

\[
V_{\mathrm{population}}(t)=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\left[
\underbrace{\operatorname{Var}_{c}\big(\mu_{n,c}(t)\big)}_{\text{differences between condition means}}
+
\underbrace{\operatorname{Mean}_{c}
\operatorname{Var}_{r}\big(x_{n,c,r}(t)\big)}_{\text{trial-to-trial variability}}
\right].
\]

Here, \(c\) indexes conditions and \(r\) indexes trials. Conditions receive equal weight. **The trial variance is not divided by the number of trials**—this measures individual-trial variability, not uncertainty in the trial average.

For a balanced action × task design, the numerator simplifies to

\[
E_{k,\mathrm{action}}(t)
=\frac{\left[w_k^\top(\mu_L(t)-\mu_R(t))\right]^2}{4}.
\]

More generally, the code calculates the unique action contribution by comparing regression models with and without action.

**Why the new value can be much smaller:** there is an exact relationship,

\[
\boxed{
\text{New fraction}
=
\text{Previous within-axis action fraction}
\times
\text{Fraction of population variance on that axis}
}
\]

For example, if action explains **40% of axis 1’s variance**, but axis 1 contains only **2% of population variance**, the new value is

\[
0.40\times0.02=0.008=\boxed{0.8\%}.
\]

That is mathematically consistent. LDA seeks strong separation relative to variability; it does not seek an axis containing the most population variance.

The summary bar uses \(\sum_t E(t)/\sum_t V_{\mathrm{population}}(t)\). It does not include the variance of a shared temporal response as a separate component.

If you intended **“what fraction of the population’s condition-dependent mean activity is explained?”**, that is a different denominator: only the first term above, excluding trial-to-trial variability. The current plots include both terms.

Created [earlyLearningEmbedding/example_earlyLearningEmbedding.m](./analysis/earlyLearningEmbedding/example_earlyLearningEmbedding.m).

It:
- Reuses stimulus/reward extraction and optional z-scoring.
- Creates **30 overlapping chunks per animal and stimulus**, targeting **⅓ window overlap**.
- Concatenates neurons across animals into one shared UMAP.
- Shows three panels colored by **S1/S2**, **learning chunk**, and **stimulus/reward**.

Each point represents a **chunk average**: 120 points total by default. Exact trial ranges, retained neurons, and excluded animals are recorded in the output.

Main settings:
```matlab
cfg.nBins = 30;
cfg.overlap = 1/3;
cfg.response.stimulusOffsets = 1:3;
cfg.response.rewardOffsets = 3:4;
```

Eight tests passed, including real UMAP fits; synthetic-data figures were visually checked.

Created [fn_compareFolders.m](./helper_function/fn_compareFolders.m).

```matlab
[summary, details] = fn_compareFolders( ...
    'F:\folder1', 'G:\folder2', ...
    'CompareContents', true);
```

Recursively compares files and subfolders, prints statistics and differences, and returns:

- `summary.allMatch`: whether everything matches.
- `summary`: counts, total bytes, mismatches, and errors.
- `details.files` / `.folders`: results by relative path.

`CompareContents=true` checks bytes. Default is faster **path-and-size comparison only**.

Both folders remain unchanged. All four tests passed.

> also let me know if there are dependencies of this function

`fn_compareFolders` has only two dependencies:

- **MATLAB built-in functions**—no additional toolboxes or repository functions.
- **MATLAB’s Java JVM**, used to resolve folder paths and detect repeated directory targets. Standard MATLAB sessions include it; avoid starting MATLAB with `-nojvm`.

Check Java availability with:
```matlab
usejava('jvm')   % Should return true
```

All helper functions are inside `fn_compareFolders.m`, so you can copy that single file elsewhere. Tested in **MATLAB R2023b**.

> how do you get this trial-by-trial distribution when the trials identity are different across animals

These are **pseudo-trials**, not simultaneously recorded trials across animals.

For each condition separately—for example, S1:

1. Randomly select one S1 trial from each animal.
2. Concatenate their neural-response vectors:
   \[
   x_{\text{pseudo}}=
   \begin{bmatrix}
   x_{\text{animal 1, trial }i}\\
   x_{\text{animal 2, trial }j}\\
   \vdots
   \end{bmatrix}
   \]
3. Project that combined vector onto each LDA axis:
   \[
   z_k=w_k^\top x_{\text{pseudo}}.
   \]

The trials share the **condition**, but their trial numbers, sessions, and learning positions need not match.

With the default `nPseudoTrials=[]`, the number of points per condition equals the **smallest available trial count across retained animals**. Trials are sampled without replacement within each animal. Setting a number instead samples with replacement.

This preserves trial variability and correlations **within each animal**, while assuming independent trial noise **between animals**. Therefore, the scatter represents a synthetic pooled-population distribution—not observed simultaneous population trials. It can look more separable because independent noise partly averages out across animals.

**The LDA axes themselves are fitted using the original animals’ means and covariances**, not the sampled pseudo-trials. Changing the sampling seed changes the plotted points, not the axes.

You can inspect each point’s original trial indices:
```matlab
taskStim.projection.sourceTrial{conditionIndex}
taskStim.projection.animalIndices
```
Each row is one plotted point; each column identifies the contributing trial from one retained animal.

> If I want to find coordinated changes of two brain areas over learning, what analysis should I use

<details><summary>6 previous messages</summary>

> I’ll distinguish changes in average responses from trial-to-trial coupling, since they require different analyses, and check the relevant methods literature.
>
> <details><summary>Searched the web</summary>
>
> Searched the web for `Semedo 2019 cortical areas interact through a communication subspace reduced rank regression neuron ...`
>
> </details>
>
> Context compaction
>
> Source: automatic
> Status: completed
>
> I have data for trial-to-trial coupling
>
> With paired trial data, reduced-rank regression is a good starting point. I’ll focus on measuring whether coupling strengthens or changes its neural pattern over learning, while separating it from changes in average responses.
>
> <details><summary>Searched the web</summary>
>
> Searched the web for `Semedo 2019 cortical areas interact through a communication subspace reduced rank regression PMC`
>
> </details>
</details>

Then I would start with **cross-validated reduced-rank regression (RRR)**, using the paired trials from both areas. It identifies population patterns in area A that predict trial-to-trial fluctuations in area B—the “communication subspace” approach used by [Semedo et al., 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6449210/).

For your learning analysis, I suggest the following design.

**1. Extract one response per neuron per trial.**

Analyze stimulus and reward periods separately, using your existing extraction:

\[
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathrm{trials}}\times n_A},
\qquad
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathrm{trials}}\times n_B}.
\]

Each row must represent **the same actual trial in both areas**. Fit within each animal; combine the resulting metrics across animals.

**2. Separate coupling from shared average responses.**

Both areas responding more strongly to S1 than S2 can create correlation without trial-to-trial coupling. Subtract the expected response for each condition within a local learning period:

\[
\widetilde x_i=x_i-\widehat{\mathbb E}[x\mid\text{condition, learning period}],
\]

and similarly for \(y_i\). Estimate these expectations using training data only.

You can additionally account for RT, movement, and reward outcome. Comparing results before and after these adjustments shows how much coupling is associated with those variables.

**3. Predict one area from the other using a few shared dimensions.**

Fit:

\[
\widetilde Y\approx\widetilde X B,
\qquad \operatorname{rank}(B)\le r.
\]

Select rank and regularization using cross-validation. Evaluate prediction on held-out trials, relative to a baseline that predicts no residual fluctuation. This gives a measure of **how much trial-to-trial variability in B is predictable from A**.

For learning, I would make these three plots:

| Plot | Question answered |
|---|---|
| Held-out prediction \(R^2\) versus learning bin | Does coupling become stronger? |
| Prediction \(R^2\) versus number of dimensions, for early and late learning | Does coupling involve more or fewer dimensions? |
| Train-bin × test-bin prediction matrix | Does the same relationship persist, or does it reorganize? |

The last plot is particularly useful: **good prediction within early and late learning, but poor early-to-late generalization, suggests that the relationship changes rather than simply strengthens.** Comparing mappings requires the same tracked neurons across those periods.

Use comparable trial counts and neuron populations across learning bins. Shuffle trial pairing **within condition and local learning period** as a control, preserving average responses while disrupting paired fluctuations.

For your data, my first analysis would therefore be **stimulus-period and reward-period RRR, separately, with within-bin prediction and cross-learning generalization**. These measure statistical coupling; shared inputs can also produce it, so prediction alone does not establish direct causal influence.

> for taskActionGeometry, this is looking promising so do the following:
> (1) make all the LDA axes (especially the 1st axes) computation cross-validated 5 fold. run multiple splits in training and test data, get projection and variance explained for each of these splits, and take the average with SEm for plotting. for the directional vector of these axes, also take the average of all splits
> (2) For the 1st LDA axes, also use it as a linear decoder (compute criterion using LDA) compute the decoding accuracy in the testing grouping. Since each neuron is treated independently, the weight of each neuron in the population decoder should be the same with single neuron decoder. in one plot, plot the decoding accuracy over time, just like the projection plot, for average single neuron decoding accuracy of top 10% neurons and top 25% neurons (cross-validated). then, in another plot, for choice decoding, pick the continuous 200ms window (3 frames) with the highest decoding accuracy in the temporal dynamics, average that, and plot 2 curves: the avg single neuron decoding accuracy of top 10%, and top 25% neuron. for task decoding, take the average decoding accuracy over the whole window

> s=s.replace('## Axis fitting: math and implementation', '''## Repeated five-fold LDA and decoding (default)
>
> `method='lda'` now defaults to `crossValidate=true`, `cvFolds=5`, and
> `cvRepeats=5`: 25 training/test splits. `cvSeed=1` gives reproducible private
> random streams without changing MATLAB's global RNG. Each condition/animal is
> partitioned independently; **all time bins of a trial stay in the same fold**.
> Training normalization, moments, all residual-axis fits, and decoder thresholds
> are learned without the outer test trials. The original in-sample behavior is
> available with `cfg.crossValidate=false`; other axis methods remain descriptive.
>
> Each training model is evaluated on its held-out trials. Projections and every
> variance/separation metric are computed per split and then averaged. Variance
> fractions are the mean of split-wise ratios, not a ratio of pooled numerators.
> These are held-out descriptive variances, not prediction R-squared and not
> noise-corrected estimates: squared differences of finite test means retain noise.
> A rank is reported only if it is estimable in every split.
>
> For a metric $q_{rf}$ from repeat $r$, fold $f$:
>
> $$q_r=F^{-1}\\sum_f q_{rf},\\quad \\bar q=R^{-1}\\sum_r q_r,
> \\quad SEM=SD(q_1,\\ldots,q_R)/\\sqrt R.$$
>
> The SEM describes sensitivity to repeated partitions of this dataset; repeated
> partitions are not independent subjects. It is **not** an animal-level or
> population-sampling confidence interval. Marginal projections are averaged
> across conditions *within each split* before computing SEM.
>
> Binary axes are oriented toward level 2 using training means. Higher residual
> ranks retain their training rank; unstable ranks may average to weak directions.
> For multiclass axes, signs align to the first training fit. Each split's weights
> are converted to raw neuron coordinates and unit normalized before averaging:
>
> $$v_s=\\frac{w_s/\\sigma_s}{\\|w_s/\\sigma_s\\|},\\qquad
> \\bar v=S^{-1}\\sum_s v_s.$$
>
> `meanWeights` stores this arithmetic average. `weights` and `weightsRaw` store
> its unit-normalized direction; similarity plots use these raw-coordinate mean
> directions. Averaging **does not preserve orthogonality** between residual ranks.
> The averaged vector is not used to score test data. The plotted projections are
> means of split projections in their respective training-normalized coordinates;
> applying the mean vector to raw data will not reproduce them. `conditionMeans`
> and the activity sanity figure remain full-data **raw** descriptive PSTHs.
>
> ### First-axis and single-neuron linear decoders
>
> Decoding applies to binary variables; multilevel factors still have geometry but
> are skipped by the decoder. Training uses equal class priors and equal weights
> for conditions within each class. With class means $\\mu_1,\\mu_2$, classify as
> level 2 when
>
> $$w^\\top x > w^\\top(\\mu_1+\\mu_2)/2.$$
>
> Single-neuron LDA uses $w_j=(\\mu_{2j}-\\mu_{1j})/(s_j^2+\\lambda)$ and midpoint
> $h_j=(\\mu_{1j}+\\mu_{2j})/2$, predicting level 2 if $w_j(x_j-h_j)>0$.
> Both weights and criteria pool the entire selected time window, just like the
> first geometry axis; they are not refitted per time point. Zero-score ties count
> as 0.5. Accuracy balances classes and conditions within classes.
>
> With `covariance='diagonal'`, the unnormalized population coefficients equal
> these single-neuron coefficients, up to a common positive scale. With
> `withinAnimal`, covariance couples neurons and this equivalence does not hold.
> The population subset decoder uses the selected entries of the first LDA axis
> and their summed midpoint criterion; it does not fit a separate axis per subset.
> This is a restricted first-axis decoder (especially relevant for full blocks).
>
> For **each outer training set**, `decoderInnerFolds=3` performs inner CV to:
>
> 1. Rank pooled neurons by their single-neuron held-out balanced accuracy,
>    averaged over the selected time window. This gives one fixed ranking per
>    variable and outer split, not a different top group at each time point.
> 2. For each percentage, take `ceil(percent * nNeurons)` from this ranking.
> 3. For `decoderChoiceVariable='action'`, select the continuous
>    `decoderWindowFrames=3` window maximizing the inner-CV **mean single-neuron**
>    accuracy for that subset. Use exactly the same window for its single-neuron
>    and population outer-test summaries. Task and other variables average accuracy
>    over the full time window. This averages accuracies, not responses before
>    decoding. Three samples represent 200 ms at 15 Hz; set the frame count for
>    other acquisition rates.
>
> The outer models are fitted on all outer training trials and evaluated only on
> outer test trials. `decoderFractions=0.05:0.05:1` sets the summary x-axis. Its two
> curves are population accuracy and mean single-neuron accuracy. The time-course
> figure uses `decoderTopFractions=[0.10 0.25]`.
>
> For multiple animals, population accuracy requires pseudo-trials: within each
> condition, independently draw `decoderPseudoTrials=200` **outer-test** trials per
> animal, with replacement. The same draws are reused for all variables/subsets;
> within-animal correlations remain intact and cross-animal noise is assumed
> independent. One animal uses all actual test trials without resampling. Recorded
> source trial numbers refer to the original input cell's third dimension.
> Single-neuron accuracies always use all actual test trials. These are within-animal
> trial generalization estimates, not generalization to new animals or sessions.
>
> `crossValidation.assignments{condition,animal}` is original trial x repeat;
> entries give the held-out fold. `crossValidation.models{s}` stores training
> weights, scaling and axis labels. `crossValidation.splits{s}` stores held-out
> projection/variance/factor metrics. `crossValidation.varianceSEM` and `factorSEM`
> contain plotted repeat SEMs. `decoding.splits{s}` stores ranks, inner-validation
> accuracy, window indices, criteria, and pseudo-trial `sourceTrial` indices.
> `decoding.mean` and `.sem` hold the aggregated curves. Ranking/window indices in
> individual splits are authoritative; their averages are descriptive only.
>
> Nested selection avoids test-set feature/window selection bias; see the
> [scikit-learn nested-CV explanation](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/auto_examples/model_selection/plot_nested_cross_validation_iris.html).
>
> ## Axis fitting: math and implementation''')
